## Appendix E Synthetic Mqar Experimental Details

In this paper, we propose Mqar as a useful tool to help explain gaps between three popular sequence modeling layers — Transformers or Sparse Transformers, Convolutions, and Recurrences. In this section, we detail the motivation behind the design of Mqar and include extended experiments on additional architectures. We provide a procedure for generating Mqar synthetic data, which can help in the development of new architectures.

### E.1 Mqar Generation Procedure

Here we provide additional discussion on the properties of our Mqar synthetic data. The objective of the synthetic analysis is to help explain the differences in language modeling behavior between different classes of language modeling architectures, as observed on real-world data Section 3 . Synthetic recall tasks were used in the development of Hyena Poli et al. [2023a] and H3 Fu et al. [2023a] , and Olsson et al. [2022] to study Transformer in-context learning behavior. 1. Attention : Attention can perform recall trivially ( Proposition 4.3 ). The bound is independent of the sequence length N N .

2. Convolutions : The gated convolution models use input-independent filters. We show in Section 4 that the dimensionality for solving recall grows with the input sequence length. Real language modeling can require performing 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ) \mathcal{O}(N) recalls in one forward pass. Our intuition is that it is difficult to efficiently compute all token-interaction distances with such a filter.

3. Recurrences : Recurrent models include a single hidden state as an information bottleneck. We show in Section H.6 that the gated recurrence requires Ω ⁡ ( N ) \Omega(N) bits to solve Mqar for d ≤ N d\leq\sqrt{N} for model dimension d d . Our intuition is that it is difficult to store large numbers of key-value pairs seen in the sequence in low-dimensional hidden states.

Motivated by this analysis, we propose Procedure 1 for generating synthetic Mqar data. This procedure allows toggling two simple properties of the synthetic dataset, specified below, to reveal the differences between the sequence modeling architecture families. We also discuss deviations from the prior work that uses synthetic recall data. 1. Size of the Key-Value Map : The number of unique key-value pairs appearing in each example in the dataset. In Procedure 1 , we use the input parameter D D to toggle this value.

2. Number of Gaps in the Data : The number of unique token-interaction distances required to perform the recall task. Prior work assumes there is a single query token that requires recall per example, failing to test whether the architecture can support multiple token-interaction distances. In Procedure 1 , we use the parameters N N (sequence length), D D (size of the key-value map per example), and α \alpha to toggle this property. We enforce that the token-interaction distances appearing in the synthetic examples follow a power-law distribution specified by α \alpha (based on Figure 3 ). Keeping α \alpha and D D constant while varying N N changes the number of unique token-interaction distances appearing in the example.

Finally, noting that the vocabulary size appears in the theoretical bounds for all three architecture families ( Section 4 ), we increase the vocabulary size to be much larger than the model dimension as typically seen in language modeling (30k - 50k tokens). Prior work uses vocab sizes ≤ 40 \leq 40 tokens.

### E.2 Training Details

We first describe the architectures evaluated on Mqar synthetic data and then the training hyperparameters. We evaluate the following four architecture categories in the experiments: 1. Attention Standard GPT-2 style multi-headed Transformer architecture with learned positional embeddings Brown et al. [2020] . Attention, the core building block of Transformers, is defined in Section 2 . The architecture for synthetics has 1 attention head.

2. Gated convolutions (Hyena Poli et al. [2023a] , RWKV Peng et al. [2023] ). Gating, convolutions, and the class of gated convolutions are defined in Section 2 .

3. Gated recurrences (RetNet Sun et al. [2023] ). RetNet proposes computing attention over chunks of the sequence and combining this with a recurrence over the sequence. We evaluate this architecture (“Chunked RNN”) with chunk sizes of 32 32 and 8 8 in Figure 6 . In RetNet, hidden states are updated as: S n = γ ​ S n − 1 + A n T ​ V n S_{n}=\gamma S_{n-1}+A_{n}^{T}V_{n} Outputs at each timestep are defined as O n = C n ​ S n O_{n}=C_{n}S_{n} where γ ∈ ℝ 1 \gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{1} , A = x ​ W a A=xW_{a} for input x ∈ ℕ × 𝕕 x\in\mathbb{N\times d} and learned weight matrix W a ∈ ℝ d × d W_{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} , C = x ​ W c C=xW_{c} for W c ∈ ℝ d × d W_{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} , V = x ​ W v V=xW_{v} for W v ∈ ℝ d × d W_{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} . RetNet similar to a state-space-model (SSM) Gu et al. [2021] , except for that the matrices A A and C C are input-dependent (i.e. they are functions of the input x x ). We note that RetNet is a special case of the recently proposed Mamba Anonymous [2023a] and GateLoop Anonymous [2023b] architectures, which replace the γ \gamma term in RetNet with yet another input-dependent matrix. The RetNet model is formally defined in Section H.2.3 .

4. Sparse attention Several works have proposed methods for efficiently computing attention Tay et al. [2022] . We evaluate two classical methods: sliding window attention Beltagy et al. [2020] , Zaheer et al. [2020] and blocked window attention Child et al. [2019b] , Qiu et al. [2019] . Consider a window size w w . Sliding window attention permits each token to attend to the prior w w tokens in the sequence. Blocked window attention first splits the sequence into blocks of size w w tokens, and then computes standard causal attention within the block.

For all synthetic runs in the main paper and the appendix, we use the following training protocol: • Optimizer and schedule : Weight decay 0.1 0.1 , warmup duration 10 % 10\% , linear warmup, AdamW optimizer. For each run, we sweep the learning rates in np . logspace ⁡ ( − 4 , − 2 , 4 ) \mathrm{np.logspace(-4,-2,4)} . We train for 64 64 epochs.

• Training duration : The global batch size is 8 8 for input sequence length or model dimension ≥ 512 \geq 512 , 16 16 for input sequence length or model dimension ≥ 256 \geq 256 , and 64 64 otherwise.

• Width and depth : For all synthetic experiments, we use exactly two layers (each with one sequence mixer and one MLP, interleaved with layer normalization). The model dimension, sequence length, and number of KV pairs are varied based on the relevant experiment (see Appendix F ).

• Position information : No position embeddings are used for the pure convolution runs (input-dependent nor input-independent filters). Position embeddings are used for the runs with any attention variant.

• Data : We train and evaluate each model on 100,000 100,000 and 3,000 3,000 data points respectively. The data and data order for all runs are constant.

## Appendix F Extended Results on Mqar Across Architectures

In this section, we provide further validation that Mqar is a useful diagnostic tool to explain the behaviors of a wide variety of architecture families. We show: 1. Gated convolutions In the main paper, we claim the gated convolution models with input-independent filters use larger dimensionality than attention as the number of token-interaction distances required for the task increases ( N N , holding α \alpha and D D constant in Procedure 1 ).

2. Gated recurrences In this section, we show gated recurrrent models use larger dimensionality than attention to solve the task as the number of unique key-value pairs to store ( D D in Procedure 1 ) increases.

3. Sparse attention In this section, we validate that sliding window attention helps close the Mqar gap when the token-interaction distances are relatively short, and degrades on longer distances. Meanwhile, blocked window attention struggles to close the gap (intuitively tokens earlier in a block are able to interact with few other tokens in the sequence). Concretely, in Appendix D , we observe that repeated bigrams in the Pile and RedPajama corpora tend to occur within neighboring tokens in the context.

We finally show the architectures that perform well on Mqar also perform well on downstream real-world AR on the Pile. We use the experimental protocols outlined in Appendix E for the synthetic experiments and Appendix C for the downstream experiments in this section.

### F.1 Synthetic Experiments

##### Experiment 1: Increasing the number of token-interaction distances per example.

To construct the synthetic dataset following Procedure 1 , we set α = 0.1 \alpha=0.1 and | C | = 8,192 |C|=8,192 for all experiments. We vary N ∈ { 64,128,256,512 } N\in\{64,128,256,512\} to increase the number of token-interaction distances that will appear in the examples and we vary the model dimension ∈ { 64,128,256,512 } \in\{64,128,256,512\} . The results of this experiment are shown in Fig. 2 , validating that the gated convolutiosn use larger dimensionality than attention as the number of token-interaction distances required for the task increases.

##### Experiment 2: Increasing the number of key-value pairs occurring per example.

To construct the synthetic dataset following Procedure 1 , we set α = 0.1 \alpha=0.1 and | C | = 8,192 |C|=8,192 for all experiments. We fix N = 256 N=256 and vary D ∈ { N 32 , N 16 , N 8 , N 4 } D\in\{\frac{N}{32},\frac{N}{16},\frac{N}{8},\frac{N}{4}\} to increase the number of key-value pairs occurring per example. For each number of key-value pairs per example, we additionally vary the model dimension ∈ { 64,128,256 } \in\{64,128,256\} . The results are shown in Fig. 6 . We validate that as the number of associative recall keys and values exceeds the window size, the model again uses increased dimensionality relative to full O ⁡ ( N 2 ) O(N^{2}) attention to solve the task. While Fig. 6 highlights RetNet, we note that it is a special case of the recently released Mamba Anonymous [2023a] and GateLoop Anonymous [2023b] architectures. Corresponding to this experiment, we theoretically show in Section H.6 that the gated recurrence uses Ω ⁡ ( N ) \Omega(N) bits to solve Mqar for d ≤ N d\leq\sqrt{N} . Intuitively, it is difficult to store a large number of key-value pairs in low dimensional hidden states.

##### Experiment 3: Increasing the range of token-interaction distancs per example.

To construct the synthetic dataset following Procedure 1 , we set α = 0.1 \alpha=0.1 and | C | = 8,192 |C|=8,192 for all experiments. We fix N = 256 N=256 and vary D ∈ { N 32 , N 16 , N 8 , N 4 } D\in\{\frac{N}{32},\frac{N}{16},\frac{N}{8},\frac{N}{4}\} to increase the number of key-value pairs occurring per example and the range of token-interaction distances per example. For each number of key-value pairs per example, we additionally vary the model dimension ∈ { 64,128,256 } \in\{64,128,256\} .

The results are shown in Fig. 6 , validating that sliding window attention closes the gap to attention when the number of KV pairs is within the window size. I.e., 16 16 KVs means that there are 16 16 keys and 16 16 values, for 32 32 total tokens. Sliding attention with window size 32 32 performs well up until 16 16 KVs and degrades beyond this point relative to attention models of the same dimensionality. Sliding window attention with window size 8 8 does not perform well on any setting, as expected. Blocked attention also not perform well – intuitively tokens at the beginning of a block lack sufficient prior context to attend to.

### F.2 Demonstrating Mqar as a Tool for Architecture Development

In the main paper, we provide experiments showing that there is gap between the gated convolution and attention models on synthetic Mqar data and correspondingly in the downstream Pile results. In Table 4 , we show that the trends of RetNet and sparse attention on Mqar also carry to the downstream results. We hope Mqar synthetic data may be useful in future architectural development.

## Appendix G Mqar Perplexity Gap and Model Size

In this section, we investigate how the associative recall gap changes as we increase the model size to a billion parameters and beyond. Overall, the results suggest that the larger models improve in memorizing bigrams from the training data, but do not rapidly improve in in-context learning (perplexity on rare bigrams).

Below, we pretrain at the 1.4Bn parameter scale and observe that the 70M parameter attention model is a full perplexity point better on AR than this 1.4Bn Hyena model (Table 1 ). We discuss these results further in ( Section 3 ).

##### Open-source 7B parameter models

We next evaluate the RWKV (gated convolution) Peng et al. [2023] and Llama 2 (attention) pretrained models at the 7B parameter scale Touvron et al. [2023] . 9 9 9 We specifically evaluate the RWKV-Raven model downloaded from https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/model_doc/rwkv and the Llama 2 model downloaded from https://github.com/facebookresearch/llama . These are both popular models that took a significant amount of effort to train, towards maximizing quality. We find that there is a gap between RWKV and attention at the 7B scale and that it increases as the model needs to conduct more recalls per sequence ( P P below).

We summarize the experimental protocol below. 1. Justifying the experimental protocol. Since frequent bigrams may just be memorized by the model and not require in-context recall, our work measures AR quality on infrequent bigrams in validation sequences ( Figure 1 , Section C.1 ). We do not have access to the custom training data mixtures used in training RWKV or Llama 2 to measure bigram frequencies, so we use a synthetic test to fairly measure the AR capabilities.

2. Evaluation dataset. We construct the synthetic dataset following Algorithm 1 , where each sequence contains P P token pairs, or “bigrams”. Each bigram, containing a “key” token and a “value” token, appears twice in the sequence. On the second occurrence of a “key” token, the model should look back to the prior occurrence to output the corresponding “value” token. We measure the AR perplexity of the model based on its ability to predict the correct “value” token as the next word for these repeated keys. The sequences are constructed using the models’ vocabulary tokens.

3. Evaluation details. We evaluate (inference only, no training) on sequence lengths of 1024 1024 tokens. We measure the AR perplexity when the sequences contain P ∈ { 16 , 32 , 64 , 128 , 256 } P\in\{16,32,64,128,256\} key-value pairs, using 1000 1000 samples per P P value. The tokens that do not contain a key or value are simply filled with a fixed token id (so this token is repeated frequently in the sequence). We plot perplexity for AR and non-AR tokens (fixed token) vs. P P . We find RWKV quality degrades with P P on the AR slice (blue line), while all other lines remain flat. MQAR remains problematic at scale.

Overall our results suggest that simply scaling the model size may not close the MQAR quality gap between the gated convolutions and attention.

##### Measuring AR Gap with Scale

In the main paper, we measure the AR perplexity on downstream data based on bigrams that are seen < 1,250 × <1,250\times during pretraining. However, we hypothesize that larger models may memorize a larger number of bigram pairs seen in the training dataset, but do not rapidly gain the capability to perform associative recall as well as attention in-context. In-context learning is defined as learning from examples provided in context [ Xie et al., 2021 ] .

Concretely, the gap between the Hyena / RWKV and attention models at the 350m scale is 1.85 / 1.84 perplexity points when we focus on bigrams seen < 1,250 × <1,250\times during pretraining ( Table 1 ). If we instead focus on bigrams seen 1 × 1\times during pretraining, the gap to attention quality is 12.0 / 13.2 perplexity points respectively. The gated convolutions appear to struggle in the regime of rare bigrams that require the model to use the context.

## Appendix H Details on Theoretical Analysis

This section provides proofs and extensions to the theoretical results in the main paper.

### H.1 Preliminaries and Notation

#### H.1.1 Notation

We denote the all 1 1 row vector of size k k , given by [ 1 1 … 1 1 ] \begin{bmatrix}1&1&\ldots&1&1\end{bmatrix} , and the all 0 0 row vector of size k k , given by [ 0 0 … 0 0 ] \begin{bmatrix}0&0&\ldots&0&0\end{bmatrix} , as 𝟏 k \bm{1}^{k} and 𝟎 k \bm{0}^{k} , respectively. We also construe the standard basis vector 𝐞 i \mathbf{e}_{i} as a column vector in these notes, and adhere to the following matrix indexing convention: 𝐌 ⁡ [ i , j ] {\bf M}[i,j] is the entry in the i i th row and the j j th column, 𝐌 [ i , : ] ∈ 𝔽 1 × n {\bf M}[i,:]\in\mathbbm{F}^{1\times n} denotes the i i th row, and 𝐌 [ : , j ] ∈ 𝔽 m × 1 {\bf M}[:,j]\in\mathbbm{F}^{m\times 1} denotes the j j th column of 𝐌 ∈ 𝔽 m × n {\bf M}\in\mathbbm{F}^{m\times n} . We then use 𝟏 m × n , 𝟎 m × n ∈ 𝔽 m × 1 \bm{1}^{m\times n},\mathbf{0}^{m\times n}\in\mathbbm{F}^{m\times 1} to denote the matrix of all 1 1 s and 0 0 s, respectively.

Next, we denote the Hadamard product of vectors 𝐮 , 𝐯 ∈ 𝔽 n {\bf u},{\bf v}\in\mathbbm{F}^{n} as 𝐮 ⊙ 𝐯 {\bf u}\odot{\bf v} ; the operation can be extended to matrices by applying the Hadamard product column-wise across the matrices. This is commonly referred to as (element-wise) gating . For vectors 𝐮 , 𝐯 ∈ 𝔽 n {\bf u},{\bf v}\in\mathbbm{F}^{n} , we also denote their linear (or acyclic) convolution as 𝐮 ∗ 𝐯 {\bf u}\ast{\bf v} and cyclic convolution as 𝐮 ⊛ 𝐯 {\bf u}\circledast{\bf v} .

##### Polynomial Notation.

Because convolution is intimately tied to operations on polynomials, it is convenient to use them to discuss the inputs and outputs of gated convolution models. Let us define maps poly , poly ∗ : 𝔽 n → 𝔽 ⁡ [ X ] / ( X n ) \operatorname{poly},\operatorname{poly}^{*}:\mathbbm{F}^{n}\to\mathbbm{F}[X]/(X^{n}) such that poly ⁡ ( 𝒖 ) \displaystyle\operatorname{poly}(\bm{u}) = ∑ i = 0 n − 1 𝒖 [ i ] X i , and poly ∗ ( 𝒖 ) = ∑ i = 0 n − 1 𝒖 [ i ] X n − 1 − i . \displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\bm{u}[i]X^{i},\text{ and }\operatorname{poly}^{*}(\bm{u})=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\bm{u}[i]X^{n-1-i}. This allows us to map between vectors and polynomial. Accordingly, we also define coeff : 𝔽 ⁡ [ X ] / ( X n + 1 ) → 𝔽 n \mathrm{coeff}:\mathbbm{F}[X]/(X^{n+1})\to\mathbbm{F}^{n} as the map converting polynomials back to vectors: coeff ⁡ ( 𝒖 ⁡ ( X ) ) = 𝒖 \mathrm{coeff}(\bm{u}(X))=\bm{u} with 𝒖 ⁡ [ i ] \bm{u}[i] defined as the coefficient in 𝒖 ⁡ ( X ) \bm{u}(X) at degree i i .

These operations allow us to interpret the convolution of vectors in terms of polynomial multiplication [ Heideman and Burrus, 1988 ] . More specifically, we have 𝒖 ∗ 𝒗 \displaystyle\bm{u}\ast\bm{v} = coeff ⁡ ( 𝒖 ⁡ ( X ) ⋅ 𝒗 ⁡ ( X ) mod X n ) , and \displaystyle=\mathrm{coeff}\left(\bm{u}(X)\cdot\bm{v}(X)\mod{X^{n}}\right),\text{ and} 𝒖 ⊛ 𝒗 \displaystyle\bm{u}\circledast\bm{v} = coeff ⁡ ( 𝒖 ⁡ ( X ) ⋅ 𝒗 ⁡ ( X ) mod X n − 1 ) . \displaystyle=\mathrm{coeff}\left(\bm{u}(X)\cdot\bm{v}(X)\mod{X^{n}-1}\right). We can similarly interpret the Hadamard product of vectors 𝒖 ⊙ 𝒗 \bm{u}\odot\bm{v} as the Hadamard product of polynomials 𝒖 ⁡ ( X ) ⊙ 𝒗 ⁡ ( X ) \bm{u}(X)\odot\bm{v}(X) : 𝒖 ⊙ 𝒗 = coeff ⁡ ( 𝒖 ⁡ ( X ) ⊙ 𝒗 ⁡ ( X ) ) = coeff ⁡ ( ∑ i = 0 n − 1 ( 𝒖 ⁡ [ i ] ⋅ 𝒗 ⁡ [ i ] ) ⋅ X i ) . \bm{u}\odot\bm{v}=\mathrm{coeff}\left(\bm{u}(X)\odot\bm{v}(X)\right)=\mathrm{coeff}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(\bm{u}[i]\cdot\bm{v}[i])\cdot X^{i}\right).

##### Arithmetic Circuit Notation.

We briefly introduce the notation of arithmetic circuits [ Volkovich, 2016 ] , the focus of Section H.5 . An arithmetic circuit 𝒞 \mathcal{C} with variables X ≜ { x 1 , x 2 , … , x n } X\triangleq\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\} over a field 𝔽 \mathbbm{F} is interpreted as a directed acyclic graph, where the input nodes are labelled by either the variables from X X or constants from 𝔽 \mathbbm{F} and the internal nodes are labelled by + + or × \times with the output being the polynomial computed at the output node.

We shall also refer to the size of the circuit as the number of nodes, the depth of the circuit as the length of the longest path between an input node and the output node, and the width of the circuit as the number of parallel operations in the circuit, or ‘wires’ which will be intersected by a horizontal ‘cut’ through the circuit. Moreover, the degree of a circuit is defined as the degree of the polynomial computed by the circuit. We summarize this with the following definition:

###### Definition H.1 .

An arithmetic circuit 𝒞 \mathcal{C} is an ( n , s , Δ , w ) (n,s,\Delta,w) -circuit if 𝒞 \mathcal{C} is an n n -variate arithmetic circuit of size s s and of depth at most Δ \Delta , and width w w .

##### Model Notation.

Now we introduce the notation we will be using for defining layers. In what follows, we denote 𝒖 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} as the model input; N N as the sequence length; L L as the number of stacked layers, indexed by ℓ \ell ; and d d as the input (embedding) dimension.

#### H.1.2 Summary of the Results

The outline of our results are as follows: In Section H.2 we introduce gated convolution models and define H3, Hyena, RWKV, RetNet, and BaseConv . In Section H.3 we introduce a set of primitive operations that BaseConv can implement. We use these as tools in the subsequent proofs. Then, in Section H.5 , we show that a general arithmetic circuit of size s s and degree at most Δ \Delta can be simulated by BaseConv . We use the above results to show all models built from gating and convolutions can be simulated by BaseConv . This helps us analyze the class of gated convolutions, beyond a specific proposal (e.g. Hyena).

Next, we study the representational power that gated convolutions and attention use to solve Mqar . In Section H.7 , we derive a BaseConv model inspired from dyadic intervals to show the dimensionality for gated convolutions (with input-independent filters) solve Mqar . Next, we analyze the dimensionality for a BaseConv model with data-dependent kernels to solve Mqar in Section H.8 .

### H.2 Gated Attention-Free Models

We now present formal definitions of gated convolution and recurrence models with respect to the 5-tuple of parameters ( N , L , d , N ′ , d ′ ) \left(N,L,d,N^{\prime},d^{\prime}\right) .

###### Definition H.2 .

An ( N , L , d , N ′ , d ′ ) − Gated Convolution Model \left(N,L,d,N^{\prime},d^{\prime}\right)-\text{\text{Gated Convolution Model}} is a stacked sequence to sequence model with L L layers such that:

1. Input and output are N × d N\times d matrices,

2. Each layer’s operations consist of element-wise gating, convolution, linear projection, and

3. All the individual gated convolution layers take in N ′ × d ′ N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime} matrices and output N ′ × d ′ N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime} matrices. We refer to the tuple ( N ′ , d ′ ) (N^{\prime},d^{\prime}) as the inner dimension of the model.

We define the Hyena, RWKV, RetNet, and BaseConv layers to make step 2 more concrete. We also assume that the input 𝒖 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} is embedded into 𝒖 ′ ∈ ℝ N ′ × d ′ \bm{u}^{\prime}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}} such that

𝒖 ′ ​ [ n , t ] = { 𝒖 ⁡ [ n , t ] if ​ n < N , t < d 0 otherwise. \bm{u}^{\prime}[n,t]=\begin{cases}\bm{u}[n,t]\ \ \text{ if }n<N,\ t<d\ \\ 0\ \ \text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

The output from the last layer 𝒛 ∈ ℝ N ′ × d ′ \bm{z}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}} is transformed into output 𝒚 ∈ R N × d \bm{y}\in R^{N\times d} by extracting the top left N × d N\times d entries in 𝒛 \bm{z} .

Next, we define the class of weight matrices that we will use in the linear projections in the models:

###### Definition H.3 .

The linear projection 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 m , m {\tt Linear}_{m,m} has its matrix representation as the weight matrix 10 10 10 I.e. 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 m , m ​ ( 𝒛 ) = 𝑾 ⋅ 𝒛 {\tt Linear}_{m,m}(\bm{z})=\bm{W}\cdot\bm{z} . 𝐖 ∈ ℝ m × m \mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m} taken to be a K-matrix for 𝐖 ∈ ( ℬ ​ ℬ ∗ ) poly- ​ log ⁡ m poly- ​ log ⁡ m \mathbf{W}\in(\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\text{poly-}\log{m}}_{\text{poly-}\log{m}} ( Definition H.17 ). Consequently, each matrix 𝐖 \mathbf{W} has 𝒪 ~ ​ ( m ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(m) parameters and runtime for matrix vector multiplication, and allows us to represent general linear transformations with low-depth linear arithmetic circuits [ Dao et al., 2020 ] . We will also need linear maps 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 m , n {\tt Linear}_{m,n} for m < n m<n , where each take the corresponding square matrices from 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 m , m {\tt Linear}_{m,m} and note that such matrices has 𝒪 ~ ​ ( n ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n) parameters and runtime for matrix vector multiplication.

###### Remark H.4 .

We note that the weight matrix 𝐖 ∈ ℝ d × d \mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} above is taken to be a dense matrix in the experiments. However, for our theoretical results, we restrict the linear projection in our models as per Definition H.3 . In the rest of the paper, unless mentioned otherwise all linear projections will follow Definition H.3 .

Next, we define three popular gated convolution models that we study in our work: Hyena Poli et al. [2023a] , RWKV Peng et al. [2023] , and RetNet Sun et al. [2023] .

#### H.2.1 The Hyena Layer

We will now outline the Hyena layer [ Poli et al., 2023b ] . Hyena takes a sequence 𝒖 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} as input and produces L + 1 L+1 projections p 1 , … , p L , v p^{1},\ldots,p^{L},v by passing 𝒚 \bm{y} though a linear layer and applying a short convolution afterwards. The algorithm then recursively performs a point-wise multiplication of the projection with the convolution of the filter h l h^{l} with the previous output. We summarize this process in Algorithm 3 .

###### Remark H.5 .

In Algorithm 3 , L L is used to denute the nuber of recursive applications of the Hadamard product and convolutions, not the number of layers Note that asymptotically, the recursive step does not make a difference.

Henceforth, we will refer to a model consisting of L L Hyena layers is a gated convolution model with associated tuple ( N , L , d , N , ( L + 1 ) ​ d ) \left(N,L,d,N,(L+1)d\right) as ( N , L , d , N , ( L + 1 ) ​ d ) − Hyena \left(N,L,d,N,(L+1)d\right)-\text{Hyena} .

#### H.2.2 The RWKV Layer

We will now describe the RWKV layer [ Peng et al., 2023 ] . RWKV is typically referred to as an RNN, but, like some other recurrent models ( e.g. S4 [ Gu et al., 2021 ] ), it can also be viewed as a convolution. Here, we present the convolutional view of RWKV. We will see that it is closely related to the Hyena layer.

RWKV takes a sequence 𝒖 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} as input and applies a short convolution. Next, it produces three projections q , k , v q,k,v by passing 𝒖 \bm{u} through a linear layer. Then, it performs a convolution sandwiched by element-wise multiplication. We summarize this process in Algorithm 5 .

In practice, the short convolution filter 𝒉 s \bm{h}_{s} is restricted to a length two filter with 𝒉 s ​ [ 0 ] = μ \bm{h}_{s}[0]=\mu and 𝒉 s ​ [ 1 ] = 1 − μ \bm{h}_{s}[1]=1-\mu , where μ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \mu\in[0,1] is learned parameter. In the RWKV paper, the short convolution is referred to as a “time shift”.

In practice, the long convolution filter 𝒉 \bm{h} is also restricted to 𝒉 [ : , t ] = e w ⁡ ( t − 1 ) \bm{h}[:,t]=e^{w(t-1)} , where w ∈ ℝ w\in\mathbb{R} is a learnable parameter that controls how quickly the magnitudes of the filter decreases as t t grows.

To summarize, the differences between Hyena and RWKV are: (1) Hyena applies the short convolution after the linear projection whereas RWKV applies it before , (2) RWKV includes non-linear activations (sigmoid and softmax) while Hyena does not, (3) RWKV and Hyena use different parameterizations for the convolutional filters, and (4) Hyena recursively performs a point-wise multiplication of the projections with the convolution filter whereas RWKV performs this operation only once (though, in practice, Hyena uses a recurrence depth of one, making them equivalent in this regard).

#### H.2.3 The Retnet Layer

In this section, we introduce the RetNet layer Sun et al. [2023] . To this end, we take an input sequence 𝒖 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} and project it using learnable weight matrices. We then compute the states 𝒛 n \bm{z}^{n} recurrently as in line 6 and the output 𝙾𝚞𝚝 [ n , : ] {\tt Out}[n,:] as in line 7 for each n ∈ [ N ] n\in[N] (see Algorithm 6 below).

#### H.2.4 BaseConv

Finally, we introduce the BaseConv here as follows: 𝐘 = ( 𝒖 ​ 𝐖 + 𝒃 1 ) ⊙ ( 𝒖 ∗ 𝒉 + 𝒃 2 ) , \mathbf{Y}=(\bm{u}\mathbf{W}+\bm{b}_{1})\odot(\bm{u}\ast\bm{h}+\bm{b}_{2}), (4) with input 𝒖 ∈ ℝ N ′ × d ′ \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}} , weight matrix 𝐖 ∈ ℝ d ′ × d ′ \mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}} and bias matrices 𝒃 i ∈ ℝ N ′ × d ′ \bm{b}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}} defining linear projections of the input sequence, and 𝒉 ∈ ℝ N ′ × d ′ \bm{h}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}} is the a set of the d ′ d^{\prime} mixed length filters.

The corresponding pseudocode for BaseConv is as follows:

Note that the convolution in Algorithm 7 is not limited to causal convolution and allows circular convolution as well. For simplicity, we use ∗ \ast here and will disambiguate when required. We will start by specifying the parameter count and complexity of a single layer of BaseConv below.

###### Proposition H.6 .

A single layer of BaseConv requires O ~ ​ ( N ′ ​ d ′ ) \tilde{O}(N^{\prime}d^{\prime}) parameters and runtime.

###### Proof.

We know the parameters and runtime for the linear part of BaseConv via Definition H.3 is 𝒪 ~ ​ ( N ′ ​ d ′ ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(N^{\prime}d^{\prime}) . Further, each convolution operation requires 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ′ ) \mathcal{O}(N^{\prime}) parameters and 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ′ ​ log ⁡ N ′ ) \mathcal{O}(N^{\prime}\log{N^{\prime}}) runtime, and we employ d ′ d^{\prime} such operations. Finally the Hadamard product step takes O ⁡ ( n ​ d ) O(nd) time. ∎

Similar to Hyena, we will refer to a model consisting of L L BaseConv layers as ( N , L , d , N ′ , d ′ ) − BaseConv \left(N,L,d,N^{\prime},d^{\prime}\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} . In our experiments, we extend BaseConv by adding an MLP after Algorithm 7 . For simplicity we will denote BaseConv ​ ( 𝒖 , 𝒉 , 𝒃 1 , 𝒃 2 ) \textsc{BaseConv}\left({\bm{u},\bm{h},\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2}}\right) as BaseConv ​ ( 𝒖 , 𝒉 ) \textsc{BaseConv}\left({\bm{u},\bm{h}}\right) when 𝒃 1 = 𝒃 2 = 𝟎 \bm{b}_{1}=\bm{b}_{2}={\bf 0} .

We will now show that there exists a BaseConv model that can emulate each of the basic operations in Algorithm 7 .

###### Lemma H.7 .

The functions Linear d , d ​ ( 𝐮 ) \texttt{Linear}_{d,d}\left(\bm{u}\right) ( Definition H.3 ), with d , d ′ d,d^{\prime} defined as in Algorithm 7 , convolution with filter 𝐡 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{h}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} , and element-wise gating can be computed with Algorithm 7 via a ( N , 1 , d , N , d ′ ) − BaseConv \left(N,1,d,N,d^{\prime}\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} .

###### Proof.

For each operation from Definition H.2 and Algorithm 7 : 1. For any input 𝒖 ∈ ℝ N ′ × d ′ \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}} , Linear d , d ′ ​ ( 𝒖 ) \texttt{Linear}_{d,d^{\prime}}\left(\bm{u}\right) with matrix representation 𝑾 ∈ ℝ N ′ × d ′ \bm{W}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}} can be performed by a single BaseConv layer computing BaseConv ​ ( 𝒚 , 𝑾 , 𝒉 , 𝒃 1 , 𝒃 2 ) \textsc{BaseConv}\left({\bm{y},\bm{W},\bm{h},\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2}}\right) with 𝒃 1 \bm{b}_{1} and 𝒃 2 \bm{b}_{2} being the matrix of all 0 0 s and all 1 1 s, respectively while and the convolution with the zero filter. That is, we have 𝐘 = ( 𝒖 ​ 𝐖 + 𝟎 N ′ × d ′ ) ⊙ ( 𝒖 ∗ 𝟎 N ′ × d ′ + 𝟏 N ′ × d ′ ) = ( 𝒖 ​ 𝐖 ) ⊙ 𝟏 N ′ × d ′ = 𝒖 ​ 𝐖 = Linear d , d ′ ​ ( 𝒖 ) . \mathbf{Y}=(\bm{u}\mathbf{W}+\mathbf{0}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}})\odot(\bm{u}\ast\bm{0}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}}+\mathbf{1}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}})=(\bm{u}\mathbf{W})\odot\mathbf{1}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}}=\bm{u}\mathbf{W}=\texttt{Linear}_{d,d^{\prime}}\left(\bm{u}\right).

2. For any input 𝒖 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} , convolution with filter 𝒉 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{h}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} can be performed by a single BaseConv layer computing BaseConv ​ ( 𝒚 , 𝑾 , 𝒉 , 𝒃 1 , 𝒃 2 ) \textsc{BaseConv}\left({\bm{y},\bm{W},\bm{h},\bm{b}_{1},\bm{b}_{2}}\right) where 𝐖 , 𝒃 2 {\bf W},\bm{b}_{2} are all zeroes, and 𝒃 1 \bm{b}_{1} is the matrix of all 1s so that we get 𝐘 = ( 𝒖 ​ 𝟎 N ′ × d ′ + 𝟏 N ′ × d ′ ) ⊙ ( 𝒖 ∗ 𝒉 + 𝟎 N ′ × d ′ ) = 𝟏 N ′ × d ′ ⊙ ( 𝒖 ∗ 𝒉 ) = 𝒖 ∗ 𝒉 . \mathbf{Y}=(\bm{u}\mathbf{0}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}}+\mathbf{1}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}})\odot(\bm{u}\ast\bm{h}+\mathbf{0}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}})=\mathbf{1}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}}\odot(\bm{u}\ast\bm{h})=\bm{u}\ast\bm{h}.

3. We may compute element-wise gating between matrices 𝒖 , 𝒗 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u},\bm{v}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} , where 𝒗 \bm{v} is some fixed factor, with a single layer computing BaseConv ( 𝒚 , , ) 𝟎 N ′ × d ′ , 𝒆 0 , 𝒗 , 𝟎 N ′ × d ′ \textsc{BaseConv}\left({\bm{y},,}\right)\mathbf{0}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}},{\bm{e}_{0},\bm{v},\mathbf{0}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}}} where 𝒆 1 \bm{e}_{1} is the identity filter, respectively, by Definition H.2 . 𝐘 = ( 𝒖 ​ 𝟎 N ′ × d ′ + 𝒗 ) ⊙ ( 𝒖 ∗ 𝒆 0 + 𝟎 N ′ × d ′ ) = 𝒗 ⊙ 𝒖 . \mathbf{Y}=(\bm{u}\mathbf{0}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}}+\bm{v})\odot(\bm{u}\ast\bm{e}_{0}+\mathbf{0}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}})=\bm{v}\odot\bm{u}.

∎

### H.3 Primitives

In this section, we will establish some additional basic primitives that we expect a gated convolution model to emulate: shift , remember \texttt{shift},\texttt{remember} and add . We specify them below: 1. Shift an sequential input of length N N up or down by s s entries:

shift_up( 𝒚 , s \bm{y},s ), shift_down( 𝒚 , s \bm{y},s ) • Input: 𝒚 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{y}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} , s ≥ 0 s\geq 0 • Output: 𝒛 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{z}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} where 𝒛 + = shift_down ​ ( 𝒚 , s ) \bm{z}^{+}=\texttt{shift\_down}(\bm{y},s) and 𝒛 − = shift_up ​ ( 𝒚 , s ) \bm{z}^{-}=\texttt{shift\_up}(\bm{y},s) 𝒚 ≡ ( ← 𝒚 0 → ← 𝒚 i − 1 → ← 𝒚 i → ← 𝒚 N − 1 → ) 𝒛 + ≡ ( ← 𝟎 → ← 𝟎 → ← 𝒚 0 → ← 𝒚 N − 1 − s → ) 𝒛 − ≡ ( ← 𝒚 s → ← 𝒚 N − 1 → ← 𝟎 → ← 𝟎 → ) \bm{y}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\leftarrow\bm{y}_{0}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{y}_{i-1}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{y}_{i}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{y}_{N-1}\rightarrow\end{pmatrix}\qquad\bm{z}^{+}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\leftarrow\bm{0}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{0}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{y}_{0}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{y}_{N-1-s}\rightarrow\end{pmatrix}\qquad\bm{z}^{-}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\leftarrow\bm{y}_{s}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{y}_{N-1}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{0}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{0}\rightarrow\end{pmatrix}

2. Add a sequence 𝒙 ∈ ℝ n × d \bm{x}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d} to a running sum 𝑺 ∈ ℝ n × d \bm{S}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d} for some 2 ​ n ≤ N ′ 2n\leq N^{\prime} with both 𝒙 \bm{x} and 𝑺 \bm{S} contained as subvectors in 𝒚 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{y}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} . add n ( 𝒚 : 𝒙 , 𝑺 ) \texttt{add}_{n}(\bm{y}:\bm{x},\bm{S}) : • Input: sequence 𝒚 \bm{y} containing 𝒙 , 𝑺 ∈ ℝ n × d \bm{x},\bm{S}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d} for 2 ​ n ≤ N 2n\leq N such that 𝒚 [ 0 : n − 1 ] ≡ 𝒙 , 𝒚 [ n : 2 n − 1 ] ≡ 𝑺 \bm{y}[0:n-1]\equiv\bm{x},\bm{y}[n:2n-1]\equiv\bm{S} and 𝒚 [ 2 n : N − 1 ] ≡ 𝟎 N − 2 ​ n \bm{y}[2n:N-1]\equiv\bm{0}^{N-2n} . • Output: 𝒛 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{z}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} containing the sum 𝒚 + 𝑺 \bm{y}+\bm{S} such that 𝒚 [ 0 : n − 1 ] ≡ 𝟏 n , 𝒚 [ n : 2 n − 1 ] ≡ 𝑺 + 𝒙 \bm{y}[0:n-1]\equiv\bm{1}^{n},\bm{y}[n:2n-1]\equiv\bm{S}+\bm{x} and 𝒛 [ 2 n : N − 1 ] ≡ 𝟎 N − 2 ​ n \bm{z}[2n:N-1]\equiv\bm{0}^{N-2n} . 𝒚 ≡ ( ← 𝒙 → ← 𝑺 → ⟵ 𝟎 ⟶ ← 𝟎 → ) 𝒛 ≡ ( ← 𝟏 n → ← 𝑺 + 𝒙 → ⟵ 𝟎 ⟶ ← 𝟎 → ) \bm{y}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\leftarrow\bm{x}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{S}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \longleftarrow\bm{0}\longrightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{0}\rightarrow\\ \end{pmatrix}\qquad\bm{z}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\leftarrow\bm{1}^{n}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{S}+\bm{x}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \longleftarrow\bm{0}\longrightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{0}\rightarrow\\ \end{pmatrix}

3. Remember 𝒗 ∈ ℝ m × d \bm{v}\in\mathbbm{R}^{m\times d} as part of a sequence of input 𝒚 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{y}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} while performing gated convolution only on 𝒙 ∈ ℝ n × d \bm{x}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d} for some m , n ≤ N × d m,n\leq N\times d . remember n , m , s , t ( 𝒚 : 𝒙 , 𝒗 , 𝒉 , 𝒑 ) \texttt{remember}_{n,m,s,t}(\bm{y:x,v,h,p}) : • Input: sequence 𝒚 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{y}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} containing 𝒙 ∈ ℝ n × d , 𝒗 ∈ ℝ m × d \bm{x}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d},\bm{v}\in\mathbbm{R}^{m\times d} , and modifiers 𝒑 , 𝒉 ∈ ℝ n × d \bm{p,h}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d} such that 𝒚 [ 0 : n − 1 ] ≡ 𝒙 , 𝒚 [ n + s : n + s + m − 1 ] ≡ 𝒗 \bm{y}[0:n-1]\equiv\bm{x},\bm{y}[n+s:n+s+m-1]\equiv\bm{v} and 𝒚 ⁡ [ i ] = 0 \bm{y}[i]=0 otherwise with 𝒙 ∗ 𝒉 ∈ ℝ ( n + s ) × d \bm{x}\ast\bm{h}\in\mathbbm{R}^{(n+s)\times d} and 𝒗 ∗ 𝒉 ∈ ℝ ( m + t ) × d \bm{v}\ast\bm{h}\in\mathbbm{R}^{(m+t)\times d} . • Output: 𝒛 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{z}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} containing ( 𝒑 ⊙ ( 𝒙 ∗ 𝒉 ) ) ∈ ℝ ( n + s ) × d \left(\bm{p}\odot(\bm{x}\ast\bm{h})\right)\in\mathbbm{R}^{(n+s)\times d} , such that: 𝒚 ≡ ( ← 𝒙 → 𝟎 s ← 𝒗 → 𝟎 ) 𝒛 ≡ ( ← 𝒑 ⊙ ( 𝒙 ∗ 𝒉 ) → ← 𝒗 → 𝟎 ) \bm{y}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\leftarrow\bm{x}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{s}\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{v}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\qquad\bm{z}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\leftarrow\bm{p}\odot(\bm{x}\ast\bm{h})\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \leftarrow\bm{v}\rightarrow\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}\end{pmatrix}

These primitives are building blocks of our proofs in the sequel. We will show that each of these primitives can be solved by some ( N , L , d , N ′ , d ′ ) − BaseConv \left(N,L,d,N^{\prime},d^{\prime}\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} model with a small constant L L .

###### Proposition H.8 (The Shift Primitive) .

For any 𝐲 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{y}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} , there exist ( N , 1 , d , N , d ) − BaseConv \left(N,1,d,N,d\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} and ( N , 3 , d , N , d ) − BaseConv \left(N,3,d,N,d\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} that computes shift_down ​ ( 𝐲 , s ) \texttt{shift\_down}({\bm{y}},{s}) and shift_up ​ ( 𝐲 , s ) \texttt{shift\_up}({\bm{y}},{s}) for any s ≤ N s\leq N .

###### Proof.

Define the following kernel dependent on s ≤ N s\leq N 𝒉 s [ n , : ] ≡ { 𝟏 d if ​ n = s + 1 𝟎 d otherwise . \bm{h}_{s}[n,:]\equiv\begin{cases}\bm{1}^{d}&\text{if }n=s+1\\ \bm{0}^{d}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases} We now deal with the down and up shifts separately: 1. We define 𝐖 := 𝟎 N × d , 𝒃 1 := 𝟏 N × d , 𝒃 2 := 𝟎 N × d {\bf W}:=\mathbf{0}^{N\times d},\bm{b}_{1}:=\mathbf{1}^{N\times d},\bm{b}_{2}:=\mathbf{0}^{N\times d} . Then, for input 𝒚 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{y}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} , BaseConv ​ ( 𝒚 , 𝟎 N × d , 𝒉 s , 𝟏 N × d , 𝟎 N × d ) \textsc{BaseConv}\left({\bm{y},\mathbf{0}^{N\times d},\bm{h}_{s},\mathbf{1}^{N\times d},\mathbf{0}^{N\times d}}\right) for BaseConv in Algorithm 7 is given by equation 4 as 𝐘 ≡ 𝒚 ∗ 𝒉 s . \displaystyle{\bf Y}\equiv\bm{y}\ast\bm{h}_{s}. Now, to perform shift_down ( 𝒚 , s ) (\bm{y},s) , we note that 𝐘 [ : , j ] = 𝒚 [ : , j ] ∗ 𝒉 s [ : , j ] \displaystyle{\bf Y}[:,j]=\bm{y}[:,j]\ast\bm{h}_{s}[:,j] = coeff ( 𝒚 [ : , j ] ( X ) ⋅ 𝒉 s [ : , j ] ( X ) ) \displaystyle=\mathrm{coeff}(\bm{y}[:,j](X)\cdot\bm{h}_{s}[:,j](X)) = coeff ⁡ ( ( ∑ i = 0 N − 1 𝒚 ⁡ [ i , j ] ⋅ X i ) ⋅ X s mod X N ) \displaystyle=\mathrm{coeff}\left(\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\bm{y}[i,j]\cdot X^{i}\right)\cdot X^{s}\mod{X^{N}}\right) = coeff ⁡ ( ∑ i = 0 N − 1 𝒚 ⁡ [ i , j ] ⋅ X i + s mod X N ) \displaystyle=\mathrm{coeff}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\bm{y}[i,j]\cdot X^{i+s}\mod{X^{N}}\right) = coeff ⁡ ( ∑ i = s N − 1 + s 𝒚 ⁡ [ i − s , j ] ⋅ X i mod X N ) \displaystyle=\mathrm{coeff}\left(\sum_{i=s}^{N-1+s}\bm{y}[i-s,j]\cdot X^{i}\mod{X^{N}}\right) = coeff ⁡ ( ∑ i = s N − 1 𝒚 ⁡ [ i − s , j ] ⋅ X i ) , \displaystyle=\mathrm{coeff}\left(\sum_{i=s}^{N-1}\bm{y}[i-s,j]\cdot X^{i}\right), which implies that we exactly get what is specified in the output.

2. We again define 𝐖 := 𝟎 N × d , 𝒃 1 := 𝟏 N × d , 𝒃 2 := 𝟎 N × d {\bf W}:=\mathbf{0}^{N\times d},\bm{b}_{1}:=\mathbf{1}^{N\times d},\bm{b}_{2}:=\mathbf{0}^{N\times d} . Then, for input 𝒚 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{y}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} , BaseConv ​ ( 𝒚 , 𝟎 N × d , 𝒆 0 , 𝟏 N × d , 𝟎 N × d ) \textsc{BaseConv}\left({\bm{y},\mathbf{0}^{N\times d},\bm{e}_{0},\mathbf{1}^{N\times d},\mathbf{0}^{N\times d}}\right) for BaseConv in Algorithm 7 is given in equation 4 as 𝐘 0 ≡ 𝒚 ⊛ 𝒆 0 . \displaystyle{\bf Y}_{0}\equiv\bm{y}\circledast\bm{e}_{0}. Now, to perform shift_up ( 𝒚 , s ) (\bm{y},s) , as before, we first apply the circular convolution to reverse the input 𝐘 0 [ : , j ] = 𝒚 [ : , j ] ⊛ 𝒆 0 \displaystyle{\bf Y}_{0}[:,j]=\bm{y}[:,j]\circledast\bm{e}_{0} = coeff ⁡ ( ∑ i = 0 N − 1 𝒚 ⁡ [ N − 1 − i , j ] ⋅ X i ) , \displaystyle=\mathrm{coeff}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\bm{y}[N-1-i,j]\cdot X^{i}\right), We then apply 𝒀 1 ≡ shift_down ​ ( 𝒀 0 , s ) \bm{Y}_{1}\equiv\texttt{shift\_down}(\bm{Y}_{0},s) to get 𝒀 1 [ : , j ] \displaystyle\bm{Y}_{1}[:,j] ≡ coeff ⁡ ( ∑ i = s N − 1 𝒀 0 ​ [ N − 1 − ( i − s ) , j ] ⋅ X i ) , \displaystyle\equiv\mathrm{coeff}\left(\sum_{i=s}^{N-1}\bm{Y}_{0}[N-1-(i-s),j]\cdot X^{i}\right), ≡ coeff ⁡ ( ∑ i = s N − 1 𝒀 0 ​ [ N − 1 − i + s , j ] ⋅ X i ) . \displaystyle\equiv\mathrm{coeff}\left(\sum_{i=s}^{N-1}\bm{Y}_{0}[N-1-i+s,j]\cdot X^{i}\right). Finally, we apply another circular convolution with the identity filter to replace N − 1 − i N-1-i with i i to get 𝐘 2 [ : , j ] = 𝒀 1 [ : , j ] ⊛ 𝒆 0 \displaystyle{\bf Y}_{2}[:,j]=\bm{Y}_{1}[:,j]\circledast\bm{e}_{0} = coeff ⁡ ( ∑ i = 0 N − 1 𝒚 ⁡ [ i + s , j ] ⋅ X i ) , \displaystyle=\mathrm{coeff}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\bm{y}[i+s,j]\cdot X^{i}\right), Here, we note that we can compute both of these primitives in one and three layers, respectively (see Lemma H.11 ).

∎

Now, we present a BaseConv model with two layers that implements the add n ( 𝒚 : 𝒙 , 𝑺 ) \texttt{add}_{n}({\bm{y}:\bm{x}},{\bm{S}}) , the purpose of which is to add some window of computation 𝒙 \bm{x} to a running sum 𝑺 \bm{S} .

###### Proposition H.9 (The Running Sum Primitive) .

For any 𝐱 , 𝐒 ∈ ℝ n × d \bm{x,S}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d} contained in some 𝐲 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{y}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} , there exists a ( N , 2 , d , N , d ) − BaseConv \left(N,2,d,N,d\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} that computes add n ( 𝐲 : 𝐱 , 𝐒 ) \texttt{add}_{n}({\bm{y}:\bm{x}},{\bm{S}}) for BaseConv as in Algorithm 7 .

###### Proof.

We will show this for d ′ = 1 d^{\prime}=1 and the general case follows as we will explain at the end. We now specify the two layers that we use 𝒛 1 \displaystyle\bm{z}^{1} ≡ BaseConv ​ ( 𝒚 , 𝟎 N × 1 , 𝒉 1 , 𝒃 1 1 , 𝟎 N × 1 ) ≡ 𝒃 1 1 ⊙ ( 𝒉 1 ∗ 𝒚 ) \displaystyle\equiv\textsc{BaseConv}\left({\bm{y},\mathbf{0}^{N\times 1},\bm{h}^{1},\bm{b}^{1}_{1},\mathbf{0}^{N\times 1}}\right)\equiv\bm{b}^{1}_{1}\odot\left(\bm{h}^{1}\ast\bm{y}\right) 𝒛 \displaystyle\bm{z} ≡ BaseConv ​ ( 𝒛 1 , 𝟎 N × 1 , 𝒉 2 , 𝒃 1 2 , 𝒃 1 2 ) ≡ 𝒃 1 2 ⊙ ( 𝒉 2 ∗ 𝒚 + 𝒃 2 2 ) , \displaystyle\equiv\textsc{BaseConv}\left({\bm{z}^{1},\mathbf{0}^{N\times 1},\bm{h}^{2},\bm{b}^{2}_{1},\bm{b}^{2}_{1}}\right)\equiv\bm{b}^{2}_{1}\odot\left(\bm{h}^{2}\ast\bm{y}+\bm{b}^{2}_{2}\right), where we will specify the kernels as we go along. Let us start by defining the kernel and the bias for the first layer as 𝒉 1 ≡ ( 𝒆 0 𝒆 0 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) , 𝒃 1 ≡ ( 𝟎 n 𝟏 n 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) . \bm{h}^{1}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{e}_{0}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{e}_{0}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix},\quad\quad\bm{b}_{1}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{1}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix}. Let us first compute 𝒉 1 ∗ 𝒚 \bm{h}^{1}\ast\bm{y} as follows: 𝒉 1 ​ ( X ) ⋅ 𝒚 ​ ( X ) \displaystyle\bm{h}^{1}(X)\cdot\bm{y}(X) = ( X n + 1 ) ⋅ ( 𝑺 ⁡ ( X ) ⋅ X n + 𝒙 ⁡ ( X ) ) \displaystyle=(X^{n}+1)\cdot(\bm{S}(X)\cdot X^{n}+\bm{x}(X)) = 𝑺 ⁡ ( X ) ⋅ X 2 ​ n + ( 𝑺 + 𝒙 ) ​ ( X ) ⋅ X n + 𝒙 ⁡ ( X ) . \displaystyle=\bm{S}(X)\cdot X^{2n}+(\bm{S}+\bm{x})(X)\cdot X^{n}+\bm{x}(X). We then have 𝒛 1 ≡ 𝒃 1 1 ⊙ ( 𝒉 1 ∗ 𝒚 ) ≡ ( 𝟎 n 𝟏 n 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) ⊙ ( 𝒙 𝑺 + 𝒙 𝑺 ⋯ 𝟎 n ) ≡ ( 𝟎 n 𝑺 + 𝒙 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) \bm{z}_{1}\equiv\bm{b}_{1}^{1}\odot\left(\bm{h}^{1}\ast\bm{y}\right)\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{1}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix}\odot\begin{pmatrix}\bm{x}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{S}+\bm{x}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{S}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{S}+\bm{x}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix}

##### Resetting for Next Phase.

We now use the next layer to reset for the next phase. Here, we need the first vector to be 𝟏 n \bm{1}^{n} in order to start adding the next vector. We thus use the kernel and the biases 𝒉 2 , 𝒃 1 2 , 𝒃 2 2 \bm{h}^{2},\bm{b}^{2}_{1},\bm{b}^{2}_{2} defined as 𝒉 2 ≡ ( 𝒆 0 𝟎 n 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) , 𝒃 1 2 ≡ ( 𝟏 n 𝟏 n 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) , 𝒃 2 2 ≡ ( 𝟏 n 𝟎 n 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) . \bm{h}^{2}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{e}_{0}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix},\quad\quad\bm{b}^{2}_{1}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{1}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{1}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix},\quad\quad\bm{b}^{2}_{2}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{1}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix}. Explicitly, for the second layer, we compute the result of the convolution in terms of polynomials as follows: 𝒉 2 ​ ( X ) ⋅ 𝒛 1 ​ ( X ) = 1 ⋅ ( 𝑺 + 𝒙 ) ​ ( X ) ⋅ X n = ( 𝑺 + 𝒙 ) ​ ( X ) ⋅ X n . \displaystyle\bm{h}^{2}(X)\cdot\bm{z}^{1}(X)=1\cdot(\bm{S}+\bm{x})(X)\cdot X^{n}=(\bm{S}+\bm{x})(X)\cdot X^{n}. Thus, the output for the second layer is given by 𝒛 ≡ 𝒃 1 2 ⊙ ( 𝒉 2 ∗ 𝒛 1 + 𝒃 2 2 ) ≡ ( 𝟏 n 𝟏 n 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) ⊙ ( ( 𝟎 n 𝑺 + 𝒙 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) + ( 𝟏 n 𝟎 n 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) ) ≡ ( 𝟏 n 𝟏 n 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) ⊙ ( 𝟏 n 𝑺 + 𝒙 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) ≡ ( 𝟏 n 𝑺 + 𝒙 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) . \bm{z}\equiv\bm{b}_{1}^{2}\odot\left(\bm{h}^{2}\ast\bm{z}^{1}+\bm{b}^{2}_{2}\right)\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{1}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{1}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix}\odot\left(\begin{pmatrix}\bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{S}+\bm{x}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\bm{1}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix}\right)\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{1}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{1}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix}\odot\begin{pmatrix}\bm{1}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{S}+\bm{x}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{1}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{S}+\bm{x}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\end{pmatrix}. Therefore, we have used two BaseConv layers to add 𝒙 \bm{x} to the running sum 𝑺 \bm{S} and reset for the next phase. Here, we note that the only operations we perform and are convolutions and Hadamard product and they generalize in the obvious way to d > 1 d>1 . ∎

Next, we show that a five layer BaseConv model can perform gated convolution on windows of the input (without changing the rest of the input).

###### Proposition H.10 (The Remembering Primitive) .

For any 𝐱 ∈ ℝ n × d , 𝐯 ∈ ℝ m × d \bm{x}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d},\bm{v}\in\mathbbm{R}^{m\times d} contained in some 𝐲 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{y}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} for some n + m + s + t ≤ N n+m+s+t\leq N so that for 𝐡 ∈ ℝ n × d \bm{h}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d} and 𝐩 ∈ ℝ ( n + s ) × d \bm{p}\in\mathbbm{R}^{(n+s)\times d} with 𝐱 ∗ 𝐡 ∈ ℝ ( n + s ) × d \bm{x}\ast\bm{h}\in\mathbbm{R}^{(n+s)\times d} and 𝐯 ∗ 𝐡 ∈ ℝ ( m + t ) × d \bm{v}\ast\bm{h}\in\mathbbm{R}^{(m+t)\times d} , there exists a ( N , 5 , d , N , d ) − BaseConv \left(N,5,d,N,d\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} that computes 𝚛𝚎𝚖𝚎𝚖𝚋𝚎𝚛 ( 𝐲 : 𝐱 , 𝐯 , 𝐡 , 𝐩 ) {\tt remember}(\bm{y}:\bm{x},\bm{v},\bm{h},\bm{p}) for BaseConv as in Algorithm 7 .

###### Proof.

We will again show this for d ′ = 1 d^{\prime}=1 and the general case should follow. We now specify the first two layers that we use 𝒛 1 \displaystyle\bm{z}^{1} ≡ BaseConv ​ ( 𝒚 , 𝟎 N × 1 , 𝒉 1 , 𝒃 1 1 , 𝟎 N × d ) ≡ 𝒃 1 1 ⊙ ( 𝒉 1 ∗ 𝒚 ) \displaystyle\equiv\textsc{BaseConv}\left({\bm{y},\mathbf{0}^{N\times 1},\bm{h}^{1},\bm{b}^{1}_{1},\mathbf{0}^{N\times d}}\right)\equiv\bm{b}^{1}_{1}\odot\left(\bm{h}^{1}\ast\bm{y}\right) 𝒛 2 \displaystyle\bm{z}^{2} ≡ BaseConv ​ ( 𝒛 1 , 𝟎 N × 1 , 𝒉 2 , 𝒃 1 2 , 𝟎 N × d ) ≡ 𝒃 1 2 ⊙ ( 𝒉 2 ∗ 𝒚 ) , \displaystyle\equiv\textsc{BaseConv}\left({\bm{z}^{1},\mathbf{0}^{N\times 1},\bm{h}^{2},\bm{b}^{2}_{1},\mathbf{0}^{N\times d}}\right)\equiv\bm{b}^{2}_{1}\odot\left(\bm{h}^{2}\ast\bm{y}\right), The kernel 𝒉 1 \bm{h}^{1} and the bias 𝒃 1 1 \bm{b}^{1}_{1} for the first layer are then given by 𝒉 1 ≡ ( 𝒉 𝟎 m 𝒆 s + t 𝟎 n 𝟎 n ⋯ 𝟎 n ) , 𝒃 1 1 ≡ ( 𝒑 𝟎 m + t 𝟎 n 𝟎 s 𝟏 m ⋯ 𝟎 n ) . \bm{h}^{1}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{h}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{m}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{e}_{s+t}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \end{pmatrix},\quad\quad\bm{b}_{1}^{1}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{p}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{m+t}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{s}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{1}^{m}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \end{pmatrix}. where recall that 𝒙 ∗ 𝒉 ∈ ℝ ( n + s ) × d \bm{x}\ast\bm{h}\in\mathbbm{R}^{(n+s)\times d} and 𝒗 ∗ 𝒉 ∈ ℝ ( m + t ) × d \bm{v}\ast\bm{h}\in\mathbbm{R}^{(m+t)\times d} .

We now want to first specify the result of applying the first kernel: ( 𝒉 1 ∗ 𝒚 ) \displaystyle\left(\bm{h}^{1}\ast\bm{y}\right) = coeff ⁡ ( ( 𝒉 ⁡ ( X ) + X n + m + s + t ) ⋅ ( 𝒗 ⁡ ( X ) ⋅ X n + s + 𝒙 ⁡ ( X ) ) ) \displaystyle=\mathrm{coeff}\left((\bm{h}(X)+X^{n+m+s+t})\cdot\left(\bm{v}(X)\cdot X^{n+s}+\bm{x}(X)\right)\right) = coeff ⁡ ( 𝒉 ∗ 𝒗 ⁡ ( X ) ⋅ X n + s + 𝒉 ∗ 𝒙 ⁡ ( X ) + 𝒗 ⁡ ( X ) ⋅ X 2 ​ n + 2 ​ s + m + t + 𝒙 ⁡ ( X ) ⋅ X n + m + s + t ) \displaystyle=\mathrm{coeff}\left(\bm{h}\ast\bm{v}(X)\cdot X^{n+s}+\bm{h}\ast\bm{x}(X)+\bm{v}(X)\cdot X^{2n+2s+m+t}+\bm{x}(X)\cdot X^{n+m+s+t}\right) We then have 𝒛 1 ≡ 𝒃 1 1 ⊙ ( 𝒉 1 ∗ 𝒚 ) ≡ ( 𝒑 𝟎 m + t 𝟎 n 𝟎 s 𝟏 m ⋯ 𝟎 n ) ⊙ ( 𝒉 ∗ 𝒙 𝒉 ∗ 𝒗 𝒙 𝟎 s 𝒗 ⋯ 𝟎 n ) ≡ ( 𝒑 ⊙ ( 𝒉 ∗ 𝒙 ) 𝟎 m + t 𝟎 n 𝟎 s 𝒗 ⋯ 𝟎 n ) . \bm{z}_{1}\equiv\bm{b}_{1}^{1}\odot\left(\bm{h}^{1}\ast\bm{y}\right)\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{p}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{m+t}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{s}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{1}^{m}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \end{pmatrix}\odot\begin{pmatrix}\bm{h}\ast\bm{x}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{h}\ast\bm{v}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{x}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{s}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{v}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \end{pmatrix}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{p}\odot(\bm{h}\ast\bm{x})\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{m+t}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{s}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{v}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \end{pmatrix}. We now describe the second kernel 𝒉 2 \bm{h}^{2} and the bias matrix 𝒃 1 2 \bm{b}^{2}_{1} as follows: 𝒉 2 ≡ ( 𝒆 0 𝟎 m + t 𝒆 0 𝟎 n + s 𝟎 m ⋯ 𝟎 ) , 𝒃 1 2 ≡ ( 𝟎 n + s 𝟎 m + t 𝟎 n 𝟏 n + s 𝟏 m ⋯ 𝟎 ) \bm{h}^{2}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{e}_{0}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{m+t}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{e}^{0}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n+s}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{m}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ + 2 ​ s + m + t + ( 𝒑 ⊙ ( 𝒉 ∗ 𝒙 ) ) ​ ( X ) ) ) \displaystyle\equiv\mathrm{coeff}\left(\left(X^{m+n+s+t}+1\right)\cdot\left(\bm{v}(X)\cdot X^{2n+2s+m+t}+\left(\bm{p}\odot(\bm{h}\ast\bm{x})\right)(X)\right)\right) ≡ coeff ⁡ ( 𝒗 ⁡ ( X ) ⋅ X 3 ​ n + 3 ​ s + 2 ​ m + 2 ​ t + 𝒗 ⁡ ( X ) ⋅ X 2 ​ n + 2 ​ s + m + t CLOSE \displaystyle\equiv\mathrm{coeff}(\bm{v}(X)\cdot X^{3n+3s+2m+2t}+\bm{v}(X)\cdot X^{2n+2s+m+t} + ( 𝒑 ⊙ ( 𝒉 ∗ 𝒙 ) ) ( X ) ⋅ X m + n + s + t + ( 𝒑 ⊙ ( 𝒉 ∗ 𝒙 ) ) ( X ) ) \displaystyle\quad+\left(\bm{p}\odot(\bm{h}\ast\bm{x})\right)(X)\cdot X^{m+n+s+t}+\left(\bm{p}\odot(\bm{h}\ast\bm{x})\right)(X)) Thus we have 𝒛 2 ≡ 𝒃 2 1 ⊙ ( 𝒉 2 ∗ 𝒛 1 ) ≡ ( 𝟎 n + s 𝟎 m + t 𝟎 n 𝟏 n + s 𝟏 m ⋯ 𝟎 ) ⊙ ( 𝒑 ⊙ ( 𝒉 ∗ 𝒙 ) 𝟎 m + t 𝟎 n 𝒑 ⊙ ( 𝒉 ∗ 𝒙 ) 𝒗 ⋯ 𝟎 ) ≡ ( 𝟎 n + s 𝟎 m + t 𝟎 n 𝒑 ⊙ ( 𝒉 ∗ 𝒙 ) 𝒗 ⋯ 𝟎 ) \bm{z}^{2}\equiv\bm{b}_{2}^{1}\odot\left(\bm{h}^{2}\ast\bm{z}^{1}\right)\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{0}^{n+s}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{m+t}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{1}^{n+s}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{p}\odot(\bm{h}\ast\bm{x})\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{v}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}\end{pmatrix}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{0}^{n+s}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{m+t}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{n}\\ \bm{v}\\ \hline\cr\\ \cdots\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}\end{pmatrix} Again, we note that the only operations we perform and are convolutions and Hadamard product and they generalize in the obvious way to d >
