Finally, we show that these primitives may be composed by ’stacking’ models with matching inner dimension ( N ′ , d ′ ) (N^{\prime},d^{\prime}) .

###### Lemma H.11 .

For f , g : ℝ N × d → ℝ N × d f,g:\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d}\to\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} that have ( N , L 1 , d , N ′ , d ′ ) (N,L_{1},d,N^{\prime},d^{\prime}) and ( N , L 2 , d , N ′ , d ′ ) (N,L_{2},d,N^{\prime},d^{\prime}) BaseConv models then their composition f ∘ g f\circ g has an ( N , L 1 + L 2 , d , N ′ , d ′ ) (N,L_{1}+L_{2},d,N^{\prime},d^{\prime}) BaseConv model which can be computed by performing their models in succession, or ’stacking’.

###### Proof.

This result follows from noting that for any f ⁡ ( 𝒖 ) f(\bm{u}) which requires L 1 L_{1} layers to compute and that we can compute f ∘ g ⁡ ( 𝒖 ) = g ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( 𝒖 ) ) f\circ g(\bm{u})=g(f(\bm{u})) using the BaseConv model with L 2 L_{2} layers, yielding L 1 + L 2 L_{1}+L_{2} layers in total. ∎

#### H.3.1 BaseConv -Hyena Equivalence

We show that the equivalence between BaseConv and Hyena by showing that each layer can simulate the other’s computation using a constant number of layers.

###### Proposition H.12 .

For any input 𝐮 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} and ( N , L , d , N ′ , d ) − (N,L,d,N^{\prime},d)- Hyena such that 𝐳 Hyena ≡ Hyena ⁡ ( 𝐮 ) \bm{z}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\equiv\mathrm{Hyena}(\bm{u}) with a set of filters 𝐡 ℓ \bm{h}^{\ell} and linear projections 𝐩 ℓ \bm{p}^{\ell} as per Definition H.3 for ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , there exists a ( N , 5 ​ L , d , N ′ + N , d ) (N,5L,d,N^{\prime}+N,d) - BaseConv model such that 𝐳 Hyena ≡ BaseConv ​ ( 𝐮 ) \bm{z}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\equiv\textsc{BaseConv}(\bm{u}) .

Similarly, for any input 𝐮 BaseConv ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} and ( N , L , d , N ′ , d ) − (N,L,d,N^{\prime},d)- Coyote such that 𝐳 BaseConv ≡ BaseConv ​ ( 𝐮 ) \bm{z}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}\equiv\textsc{BaseConv}(\bm{u}) with a set of filters 𝐡 ℓ \bm{h}^{\ell} for ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , there exists a series of Hyena layers such that we have Hyena ⁡ ( Hyena ⁡ ( … ​ Hyena ​ ( 𝒖 BaseConv , 𝒉 ) ) ) ⏟ L ​ layers ≡ 𝒛 BaseConv . \underbrace{\mathrm{Hyena}\left(\mathrm{Hyena}\left(\ldots\mathrm{Hyena}(\bm{u}_{\textsc{BaseConv},\bm{h}})\right)\right)}_{L\text{ layers}}\equiv\bm{z}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}.

###### Proof.

For the input 𝒖 Hyena ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} , the output of the ℓ \ell th layer 𝒛 Hyena ℓ ∈ ℝ N ′ × d ′ \bm{z}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N^{\prime}\times d^{\prime}} for Hyena is given by (see Algorithm 3 ) 𝒛 Hyena ℓ ≡ 𝒑 Hyena ℓ ⊙ ( 𝒉 l ∗ 𝒛 ℓ − 1 ) , \displaystyle\bm{z}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\equiv\bm{p}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\odot(\bm{h}^{l}\ast\bm{z}^{\ell-1}), where 𝒑 Hyena ℓ ≡ Linear ​ ( 𝒖 Hyena ) ∈ ℝ N ′ × d \bm{p}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\equiv\texttt{Linear}(\bm{u}_{\mathrm{Hyena}})\in\mathbbm{R}^{N^{\prime}\times d} . Now, using the original input 𝒖 Hyena ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} to Hyena, we define the following input for BaseConv using one layer: 𝒖 BaseConv ≡ ( 𝒖 Hyena 𝟎 ( N ′ − N ) × d 𝒖 Hyena ) \bm{u}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{u}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{(N^{\prime}-N)\times d}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{u}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\end{pmatrix} Then, we simply use the 𝚛𝚎𝚖𝚎𝚖𝚋𝚎𝚛 N , N , N ′ − N , N ′ − N ( 𝒖 BaseConv : 𝒖 Hyena , 𝒖 Hyena , 𝒉 Hyena ℓ , 𝒑 Hyena ℓ ) {\tt remember}_{N,N,N^{\prime}-N,N^{\prime}-N}(\bm{u}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}:\bm{u}_{\mathrm{Hyena}},\bm{u}_{\mathrm{Hyena}},\bm{h}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{Hyena}},\bm{p}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}) primitive for BaseConv . Consequently, this allows us to “remember” the input 𝒖 Hyena \bm{u}_{\mathrm{Hyena}} in the output of the previous BaseConv layer 𝒛 BaseConv ℓ − 1 \bm{z}^{\ell-1}_{\textsc{BaseConv}} . We then use this to retrieve 𝒑 Hyena ℓ ≡ linear ⁡ ( 𝒖 Hyena ) \bm{p}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\equiv\mathrm{linear}(\bm{u}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}) with the projection used for BaseConv given by p BaseConv ℓ ≡ Linear ​ ( 𝒛 BaseConv ℓ − 1 ) ≡ ( 𝟏 N × d 𝒑 Hyena ℓ . ) p^{\ell}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}\equiv\texttt{Linear}(\bm{z}^{\ell-1}_{\textsc{BaseConv}})\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{1}^{N\times d}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{p}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}.\end{pmatrix} Overall, the output of the ℓ \ell th layer for BaseConv is given by 𝒛 BaseConv ℓ ≡ ( 𝒑 Hyena ℓ ⊙ ( 𝒉 Hyena ℓ ∗ 𝒖 Hyena ) 𝟎 ( M − N ) × d 𝒖 Hyena ) ≡ ( 𝒛 Hyena ℓ 𝟎 ( M − N ) × d 𝒖 Hyena ) \bm{z}^{\ell}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{p}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\odot\left(\bm{h}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\ast\bm{u}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\right)\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{(M-N)\times d}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{u}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\end{pmatrix}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{z}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}^{(M-N)\times d}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{u}_{\mathrm{Hyena}}\end{pmatrix} Hence, we can reproduce the output of the ℓ \ell th layer of Hyena using five layers of BaseConv after augmenting the input and using the remembering primitive ( Proposition H.10 ) with internal dimension N ′ + N N^{\prime}+N .

Now, for the input 𝒖 BaseConv ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} , the output of the ℓ \ell th layer for BaseConv is given by z BaseConv ℓ ≡ Linear ​ ( z BaseConv ℓ − 1 ) ⊙ conv ⁡ ( h l , z BaseConv ℓ − 1 ) . \displaystyle z^{\ell}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}\equiv\texttt{Linear}(z^{\ell-1}_{\textsc{BaseConv}})\odot\mathrm{conv}(h^{l},z^{\ell-1}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}). Here, we show inductively that simply using ℓ \ell -many Hyena models recursively simulates z BaseConv ℓ z^{\ell}_{\textsc{BaseConv}} . For ℓ = 1 \ell=1 , we have Hyena ⁡ ( 𝒖 BaseConv , 𝒉 ) ≡ Linear ​ ( 𝒖 BaseConv ) ⊙ ( 𝒉 1 ∗ 𝒖 BaseConv ) ≡ 𝒛 BaseConv 1 . \mathrm{Hyena}(\bm{u}_{\textsc{BaseConv},\bm{h}})\equiv\texttt{Linear}(\bm{u}_{\textsc{BaseConv}})\odot(\bm{h}^{1}\ast\bm{u}_{\textsc{BaseConv}})\equiv\bm{z}^{1}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}. We now assume that ( ℓ − 1 ) (\ell-1) -many recursive Hyena models produce z BaseConv ( ℓ − 1 ) z^{(\ell-1)}_{\textsc{BaseConv}} . For the ℓ \ell th layer, we then have Hyena ⁡ ( Hyena ⁡ ( … ​ Hyena ​ ( 𝒖 BaseConv , 𝒉 ) ) ) \displaystyle\mathrm{Hyena}\left(\mathrm{Hyena}\left(\ldots\mathrm{Hyena}(\bm{u}_{\textsc{BaseConv},\bm{h}})\right)\right) ≡ \displaystyle\equiv\quad Hyena ⁡ ( z BaseConv ( l − 1 ) ) \displaystyle\mathrm{Hyena}\left(z^{(l-1)}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}\right) ≡ \displaystyle\equiv\quad linear ⁡ ( z BaseConv ( l − 1 ) ) ⊙ conv ⁡ ( h l , z BaseConv ( l − 1 ) ) \displaystyle\mathrm{linear}\left(z^{(l-1)}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}\right)\odot\mathrm{conv}\left(h^{l},z^{(l-1)}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}\right) ≡ \displaystyle\equiv\quad z BaseConv ℓ . \displaystyle z^{\ell}_{\textsc{BaseConv}}. ∎

### H.4 Linear Arithmetic Circuits

In this section we show the relation between linear arithmetic circuits and BaseConv . We recall a few definitions from [ Dao et al., 2020 ] .

###### Definition H.13 (Linear Arithmetic Circuit [ Bürgisser et al., 1996 ] ) .

An arithmetic circuit is called a linear arithmetic circuit if it only uses addition, subtraction and scalar multiplication. Further, every multiplication has a fixed constant from 𝔽 \mathbbm{F} as at least one of its two inputs. In other words, all gates in the circuit are linear functions of their inputs (i.e. of the form a ​ x + b ​ y ax+by for fixed constants a , b ∈ 𝔽 a,b\in\mathbbm{F} ).

###### Definition H.14 (Butterfly Matrices [ Dao et al., 2020 ] ) .

A butterfly factor of size k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 (denoted as 𝐁 k \mathbf{B}_{k} ) is a matrix of the form 𝐁 k = \mathbf{B}_{k}= [ 𝐃 1 𝐃 2 𝐃 3 𝐃 4 ] \left[\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{D}_{1}&\mathbf{D}_{2}\\ \mathbf{D}_{3}&\mathbf{D}_{4}\end{array}\right] where each 𝐃 i \mathbf{D}_{i} is a k 2 × k 2 \frac{k}{2}\times\frac{k}{2} diagonal matrix. We restrict k k to be a power of 2 .

A butterfly factor matrix of size n n with block size k k (denoted as 𝐁 k ( n ) \mathbf{B}_{k}^{(n)} ) is a block diagonal matrix of n k \frac{n}{k} (possibly different) butterfly factors of size k k : 𝐁 k ( n ) = diag ⁡ ( [ 𝐁 k ] 1 , [ 𝐁 k ] 2 , … , [ 𝐁 k ] n k ) \mathbf{B}_{k}^{(n)}=\operatorname{diag}\left(\left[\mathbf{B}_{k}\right]_{1},\left[\mathbf{B}_{k}\right]_{2},\ldots,\left[\mathbf{B}_{k}\right]_{\frac{n}{k}}\right) Finally, a butterfly matrix of size n n (denoted as 𝐁 ( n ) \mathbf{B}^{(n)} ) is a matrix that can be expressed as a product of butterfly factor matrices: 𝐁 ( n ) = 𝐁 n ( n ) ​ 𝐁 n 2 ( n ) ​ … ​ 𝐁 2 ( n ) \mathbf{B}^{(n)}=\mathbf{B}_{n}^{(n)}\mathbf{B}_{\frac{n}{2}}^{(n)}\ldots\mathbf{B}_{2}^{(n)} . Equivalently, we may define 𝐁 ( n ) \mathbf{B}^{(n)} recursively as a matrix that can be expressed in the following form: 𝐁 ( n ) = 𝐁 n ( n ) ​ [ [ 𝐁 ( n 2 ) ] 1 0 0 [ 𝐁 ( n 2 ) ] 2 ] \mathbf{B}^{(n)}=\mathbf{B}_{n}^{(n)}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\left[\mathbf{B}^{\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}\right]_{1}}&0\\ 0&{\left[\mathbf{B}^{\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}\right]_{2}}\end{array}\right] (Note that [ 𝐁 ( n 2 ) ] 1 \left[\mathbf{B}^{\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}\right]_{1} and [ 𝐁 ( n 2 ) ] 2 \left[\mathbf{B}^{\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}\right]_{2} may be different.)

From Definition H.14 , we observe that size n n butterfly factor is comprised of three vectors 𝒅 , 𝒅 + , 𝒅 − ∈ ℝ n \bm{d,d^{+},d^{-}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n} such that 𝒅 \displaystyle\bm{d} = ( diag − 1 ⁡ ( 𝐃 1 ) , diag − 1 ⁡ ( 𝐃 4 ) ) , \displaystyle=\left(\operatorname{diag}^{-1}\left({\bf D}_{1}\right),\operatorname{diag}^{-1}\left({\bf D}_{4}\right)\right), 𝒅 + \displaystyle\bm{d}^{+} = ( 𝟎 n 2 , diag − 1 ⁡ ( 𝐃 2 ) ) , and \displaystyle=\left(\bm{0}^{\tfrac{n}{2}},\operatorname{diag}^{-1}\left({\bf D}_{2}\right)\right),\text{ and } 𝒅 − \displaystyle\bm{d}^{-} = ( diag − 1 ⁡ ( 𝐃 3 ) , 𝟎 n 2 ) , \displaystyle=\left(\operatorname{diag}^{-1}\left({\bf D}_{3}\right),\bm{0}^{\tfrac{n}{2}}\right),

where diag − 1 ⁡ ( 𝐃 ) : ℝ n × n ↦ ℝ n \operatorname{diag}^{-1}({\bf D}):\mathbbm{R}^{n\times n}\mapsto\mathbbm{R}^{n} is the mapping from diagonal matrices to the vector of its diagonal entries. Let us define 𝐃 𝟏 , 𝐃 𝟐 , 𝐃 𝟑 ∈ ℝ n × n {\bf D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times n} as diag ⁡ ( 𝒅 ) , diag ⁡ ( 𝒅 + ) \operatorname{diag}\left(\bm{d}\right),\operatorname{diag}\left(\bm{d^{+}}\right) , and diag ⁡ ( 𝒅 − ) \operatorname{diag}\left(\bm{d^{-}}\right) respectively. Then we note that

𝐃 1 ≡ [ 𝐃 1 𝟎 𝟎 𝐃 4 ] 𝐃 2 ​ 𝑺 n 2 ≡ [ 𝟎 𝐃 2 𝟎 𝟎 ] 𝑺 n 2 ​ 𝐃 3 ≡ [ 𝟎 𝟎 𝐃 3 𝟎 ] {\bf D}_{1}\equiv\left[\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{D}_{1}&\mathbf{0}\\ \mathbf{0}&\mathbf{D}_{4}\end{array}\right]\qquad{\bf D}_{2}\bm{S}^{\tfrac{n}{2}}\equiv\left[\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{D}_{2}\\ \mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right]\qquad\bm{S}^{\tfrac{n}{2}}{\bf D}_{3}\equiv\left[\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\ \mathbf{D}_{3}&\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right] (5)

where 𝐒 k ∈ 𝔽 n × n {\bf S}^{k}\in\mathbbm{F}^{n\times n} is a shift matrix for i ∈ [ n / 2 ] i\in[n/2] . This gives us the following proposition:

###### Proposition H.15 .

For any powers of 2, n = k ≥ 2 n=k\geq 2 , any butterfly factor matrix 𝐁 k ( n ) {\bf B}^{(n)}_{k} is equivalent to 𝐁 k ( n ) = 𝐒 k 2 ​ 𝐃 3 + 𝐃 2 ​ 𝐒 n 2 + 𝐃 1 {\bf B}^{(n)}_{k}={\bf S}^{\tfrac{k}{2}}{\bf D}_{3}+{\bf D}_{2}{\bf S}^{\tfrac{n}{2}}+{\bf D}_{1} where 𝐃 𝟑 , 𝐃 𝟐 , 𝐃 𝟏 , 𝐒 𝐧 𝟐 {\bf D_{3},D_{2},D_{1},S^{\frac{n}{2}}} are defined as in equation 5 .

We use Proposition H.15 to show that butterfly matrices can easily be computed by BaseConv .

###### Lemma H.16 .

For any n , d ≥ 2 n,d\geq 2 , k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , and arbitrary vector 𝐱 ∈ ℝ n ​ d \bm{x}\in\mathbbm{R}^{nd} : (1) there exists a ( N , L , d , N ′ , d ′ ) − BaseConv \left(N,L,d,N^{\prime},d^{\prime}\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} that can represent 𝐁 k ( n ​ d ) ⋅ 𝒙 {\bf B}^{(nd)}_{k}\cdot\bm{x} with N = n , N ′ = 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ) N=n,N^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}(N) , L = 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) L=\mathcal{O}(1) , and d ′ = 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ) d^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}(d) , and

(2) there exists a ( N , L , d , N ′ , d ′ ) − BaseConv \left(N,L,d,N^{\prime},d^{\prime}\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} that can represent 𝐁 ( n ​ d ) ⋅ 𝒙 {\bf B}^{(nd)}\cdot\bm{x} with N = n , N ′ = 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ) N=n,N^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}(N) , L = 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ​ d ) L=\mathcal{O}(\log nd) , and d ′ = 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ) d^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}(d) .

###### Proof.

(1) Given 𝒙 ∈ ℝ n ​ d \bm{x}\in\mathbbm{R}^{nd} , construct 𝒖 ∈ ℝ n × d \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d} where 𝒙 \bm{x} is the row-major form of 𝒖 \bm{u} . We show that BaseConv can compute 𝐁 n ​ d ⋅ 𝒙 {\bf B}_{nd}\cdot\bm{x} column by column.

Let 𝐀 = 𝐒 k 2 ​ 𝐃 3 ′ , 𝐂 = 𝐃 2 ′ ​ 𝐒 n 2 {\bf A}={\bf S}^{\tfrac{k}{2}}{\bf D}^{\prime}_{3},{\bf C}={\bf D}^{\prime}_{2}{\bf S}^{\tfrac{n}{2}} , and 𝐃 = 𝐃 1 {\bf D}={\bf D}_{1} for 𝐃 i , 𝐒 k 2 ∈ ℝ n ​ d × n ​ d {\bf D}_{i},{\bf S}^{\tfrac{k}{2}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{nd\times nd} for 1 ≤ i ≤ 3 1\leq i\leq 3 as defined in Proposition H.15 . We take 𝒅 1 = 𝟏 n ​ d ​ 𝐃 , 𝒅 2 = 𝟏 n ​ d ​ 𝐂 2 , 𝒅 3 = 𝟏 n ​ d ​ 𝐀 \bm{d}_{1}=\bm{1}^{nd}{\bf D},\ \bm{d}_{2}=\bm{1}^{nd}{\bf C}_{2},\bm{d}_{3}=\bm{1}^{nd}{\bf A} , which extracts the diagonal entries of 𝐃 i {\bf D}_{i} . With this we construct 𝐃 i ′ ∈ ℝ n × d {\bf D}^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d} where 𝒅 i \bm{d}_{i} is the row major form of 𝑫 i ′ \bm{D}^{\prime}_{i} . This implies that 𝐃 i ​ 𝒙 ≡ 𝐃 i ′ ⊙ 𝒖 . {\bf D}_{i}\bm{x}\equiv{\bf D}^{\prime}_{i}\odot\bm{u}.

Then we can decompose 𝐁 n ​ d ⋅ 𝒙 {\bf B}_{nd}\cdot\bm{x} into 𝐁 n ​ d ​ 𝒙 ≡ 𝐃 1 ⊙ 𝒖 + 𝐃 2 ⊙ 𝒖 + 𝐃 3 ⊙ 𝒖 . {\bf B}_{nd}\bm{x}\equiv{\bf D}_{1}\odot\bm{u}+{\bf D}_{2}\odot\bm{u}+{\bf D}_{3}\odot\bm{u}.

By Lemma H.7 , each Hadamard product 𝐀 ⊙ 𝒖 , 𝐁 ⊙ 𝒖 , 𝐂 ⊙ 𝒖 {\bf A}\odot\bm{u},{\bf B}\odot\bm{u},{\bf C}\odot\bm{u} can be trivially be performed with a single layer BaseConv model. Let each of these model outputs be denoted 𝒚 𝟏 , 𝒚 𝟐 , 𝒚 𝟑 \bm{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}} , respectively. Finally all that remains is to compute the 𝒚 𝟏 + 𝒚 𝟐 + 𝒚 𝟑 \bm{y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}} . We achieve this using layers of add primitives 11 11 11 Recall that add n ( 𝒚 : 𝒙 , 𝑺 ) \texttt{add}_{n}(\bm{y}:\bm{x},\bm{S}) adds the subvector 𝒙 \bm{x} to 𝑺 \bm{S} for the input 𝒚 \bm{y} . : add n ( 𝒚 1 : 𝒚 1 , 𝟎 ) \displaystyle\texttt{add}_{n}(\bm{y}^{1}:\bm{y}_{1},\bm{0}) add n ( 𝒚 2 : 𝒚 2 , 𝒚 1 ) \displaystyle\texttt{add}_{n}(\bm{y}^{2}:\bm{y}_{2},\bm{y}_{1}) add n ( 𝒚 3 : 𝒚 3 , 𝒚 1 + 𝒚 2 ) , \displaystyle\texttt{add}_{n}(\bm{y}^{3}:\bm{y}_{3},\bm{y}_{1}+\bm{y}_{2}), where using by Proposition H.9 and Lemma H.11 , this requires six more layers, and we get 𝒚 3 ≡ 𝒚 𝟏 + 𝒚 𝟐 + 𝒚 𝟑 ≡ 𝐁 n ​ d ​ 𝒙 . \bm{y}^{3}\equiv\bm{y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}}\equiv{\bf B}_{nd}\bm{x}. Then we can construct the ( N , L , d , N ′ , d ′ ) − BaseConv \left(N,L,d,N^{\prime},d^{\prime}\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} as desired with L = O ⁡ ( 1 ) L=O(1) layers.

(2) From Definition H.14 , 𝐁 ( n ​ d ) = 𝐁 n ​ d ( n ​ d ) ​ 𝐁 n ​ d 2 ( n ​ d ) ​ … ​ 𝐁 2 ( n ​ d ) \mathbf{B}^{(nd)}=\mathbf{B}_{nd}^{(nd)}\mathbf{B}_{\frac{nd}{2}}^{(nd)}\ldots\mathbf{B}_{2}^{(nd)} . From (1), BaseConv can compute any butterfly matrix by simulating the log ⁡ ( n ​ d ) \log(nd) butterfly factor matrices which comprise 𝐁 ( n ​ d ) \mathbf{B}^{(nd)} . With Lemma H.11 , this creates a BaseConv with 5 ⋅ log ⁡ ( n ​ d ) = 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( n ​ d ) ) 5\cdot\log(nd)=\mathcal{O}(\log(nd)) layers. Lemma H.11

∎

Butterfly matrices comprise the kaleidoscope hierarchy, which we define below:

###### Definition H.17 (The Kaleidoscope Hierarchy [ Dao et al., 2020 ] ) .

• Define ℬ \mathcal{B} as the set of all matrices that can be expressed in the form 𝐁 ( n ) {\bf B}^{(n)} (for some n n ).

• Define ( ℬℬ ∗ ) \left(\cal{BB}^{*}\right) as the set of matrices 𝐌 {\bf M} of the form 𝐌 = 𝐌 1 ​ 𝐌 2 ∗ {\bf M}={\bf M}_{1}{\bf M}_{2}^{*} for some 𝐌 𝟏 , 𝐌 2 ∈ ℬ {\bf M_{1}},{\bf M}_{2}\in\mathcal{B} .

• Define ( ℬℬ ∗ ) w \left(\cal{BB}^{*}\right)^{w} as the set of matrices 𝐌 {\bf M} that can be expressed as 𝐌 = 𝐌 w ​ … ​ 𝐌 2 ​ 𝐌 1 {\bf M}={\bf M}_{w}\ldots{\bf M}_{2}{\bf M}_{1} , with each 𝐌 i ∈ ( ℬℬ ∗ ) ​ ( 1 ≤ i ≤ w ) . {\bf M}_{i}\in\left(\cal{BB}^{*}\right)(1\leq i\leq w). (The notation w w represents width.)

• Define ( ℬℬ ∗ ) e w \left(\cal{BB}^{*}\right)^{w}_{e} as the set of n × n n\times n matrices 𝐌 {\bf M} that can be expressed as 𝐌 = 𝐒𝐄𝐒 ⊤ {\bf M}={\bf SES}^{\top} for some e ​ n × e ​ n en\times en matrix 𝐄 ∈ ( ℬℬ ∗ ) w {\bf E}\in\left(\cal{BB}^{*}\right)^{w} , where 𝐒 ∈ 𝔽 n × e ​ n = [ 𝐈 n 0 … 0 ] ] {\bf S}\in\mathbbm{F}^{n\times en}=\begin{bmatrix}{\bf I}_{n}&0&\ldots&0\end{bmatrix}] (i.e. 𝐌 {\bf M} is the upper-left corner of 𝐄 {\bf E} ). (The notation e e represents expansion relative to n n .)

We similarly show how BaseConv can simulate any kaleidoscope matrix.

###### Lemma H.18 .

Given n , d ≥ 2 n,d\geq 2 , e > 0 e>0 for any n ​ d × n ​ d nd\times nd matrix 𝐌 ∈ ( ℬℬ ∗ ) e w {\bf M}\in\left(\cal{BB}^{*}\right)^{w}_{e} , and 𝐱 ∈ ℝ n ​ d \bm{x}\in\mathbbm{R}^{nd} there exists a ( N , L , d , N ′ , d ′ ) − BaseConv \left(N,L,d,N^{\prime},d^{\prime}\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} that can represent 𝐌 ⋅ 𝐱 {\bf M}\cdot\bm{x} with N = n , L = 𝒪 ⁡ ( w ​ log ⁡ ( e ​ n ​ d ) ) N=n,L=\mathcal{O}(w\log(end)) , N ′ = e ​ n , N^{\prime}=en, and d ′ = d \ d^{\prime}=d .

###### Proof.

By Definition H.17 , 𝐌 {\bf M} can be decomposed with respect to size e ​ n ​ d × e ​ n ​ d end\times end matrix 𝐄 = 𝐄 1 ⋅ 𝐄 2 ⋯ 𝐄 w . {\bf E}={\bf E}_{1}\cdot{\bf E}_{2}\cdots{\bf E}_{w}.

Further, any 𝐄 i ∈ ( ℬℬ ∗ ) {\bf E}_{i}\in\left(\cal{BB}^{*}\right) can be expressed as a product of 2 ​ log ⁡ e ​ n ​ d 2\log{end} butterfly factor matrices. Then by Lemma H.16 and Lemma H.11 we can compute 𝐄 i ​ 𝒙 ′ {\bf E}_{i}\bm{x^{\prime}} in by stacking 2 ​ log ⁡ e ​ n ​ d 2\log{end} ( n , d , L , e ​ n , d ) − BaseConv \left(n,d,L,en,d\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} models each with L = 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) L=\mathcal{O}(1) . Because 𝐄 {\bf E} has width w w , Lemma H.11 implies that composing with each 𝐄 i {\bf E}_{i} for 1 ≤ i ≤ w 1\leq i\leq w constructs a final model with 𝒪 ⁡ ( w ​ log ⁡ ( e ​ n ​ d ) ) \mathcal{O}(w\log(end)) layers. ∎

Finally, the kaleidoscope hierarchy is related to linear arithmetic circuits via the following result. We note that in [ Dao et al., 2020 ] it is assumed that w = s w=s , yet inspection of the proof yields the following stronger result:

###### Theorem H.19 ( [ Dao et al., 2020 ] ) .

Let 𝐌 {\bf M} be an n × n n\times n matrix such that multiplication of 𝐌 {\bf M} times an arbitrary vector 𝐮 \bm{u} can be be represented as ( n , s , Δ , w ) (n,s,\Delta,w) -linear arithmetic circuit 𝒞 \mathcal{C} . Then, 𝐌 ∈ ( ℬℬ ∗ ) 𝒪 ⁡ ( w / n ) 𝒪 ⁡ ( Δ ) {\bf M}\in\left(\cal{BB}^{*}\right)^{\mathcal{O}(\Delta)}_{\mathcal{O}(w/n)} .

We combine Theorem H.19 and Lemma H.18 to show that BaseConv can compute any linear arithmetic circuit with polylogarithmic factors in Δ \Delta .

###### Corollary H.20 .

For any ( n ​ d , s , Δ , w ) (nd,s,\Delta,w) -linear arithmetic circuit 𝒞 \mathcal{C} that can be represented by a matrix 𝐌 ∈ ℝ n ​ d × n ​ d {\bf M}\in\mathbbm{R}^{nd\times nd} multiplied by a vector 𝐱 ∈ ℝ n ​ d \bm{x}\in\mathbbm{R}^{nd} , there exists an equivalent ( n , Δ ′ , d , w , d ) − BaseConv \left(n,\Delta^{\prime},d,w,d\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} with Δ ′ = 𝒪 ⁡ ( Δ ​ log ⁡ ( w ) ) \Delta^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}(\Delta\log(w)) such that 𝐌 ​ 𝐱 = BaseConv ​ ( 𝐮 , 𝐡 ) {\bf M}\bm{x}=\textsc{BaseConv}\left({\bm{u},\bm{h}}\right) where 𝐱 \bm{x} is the row major form of 𝐮 ∈ ℝ n × d \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n\times d} .

### H.5 General Arithmetic Circuits

We are now ready to prove the result that yields the equivalency between arithmetic circuits and BaseConv .

###### Theorem H.21 .

For any ( n ​ d , s , Δ , w ) (nd,s,\Delta,w) -arithmetic circuit 𝒞 \mathcal{C} , there exists an equivalent ( N , Δ ′ , d , N ′ , d ′ ) − BaseConv \left(N,\Delta^{\prime},d,N^{\prime},d^{\prime}\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} with N = n , Δ ′ = 𝒪 ⁡ ( Δ ​ log ⁡ w ) N=n,\Delta^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}(\Delta\log{w}) , N ′ = 𝒪 ⁡ ( w ) , d ′ = d N^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}(w),d^{\prime}=d that simulates 𝒞 \mathcal{C} .

For the reader’s convenience, we begin with a proof sketch and then provide the details afterwards.

###### Proof Sketch of Theorem H.21 .

Let us layer 𝒞 \mathcal{C} so that each layer 𝒞 ℓ \mathcal{C}_{\ell} for ℓ ∈ [ L 𝒞 ] \ell\in[L_{\mathcal{C}}] either only has linear gates or multiplication gates, where the number of such layers L 𝒞 = 𝒪 ⁡ ( Δ ) L_{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{O}(\Delta) . The composition of all 𝒞 ℓ \mathcal{C}_{\ell} layers results in 𝒞 \mathcal{C} . We use 𝒛 ℓ + 1 ∈ ℝ w \bm{z}^{\ell+1}\in\mathbbm{R}^{w} to denote the output of the ℓ \ell -th layer 𝒞 ℓ \mathcal{C}_{\ell} which feeds as the input to the ( ℓ + 1 ) (\ell+1) -th layer 𝒞 ℓ + 1 \mathcal{C}_{\ell+1} . Here, we note that if we can simulate each 𝒞 ℓ \mathcal{C}_{\ell} with BaseConv , then we can simulate the entire layered circuit 𝒞 \mathcal{C} due to Lemma H.11 .

Now, if the layer 𝒞 ℓ lin \mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{lin}} is a linear layer (with only addition gates), then it can be represented by a matrix 𝑴 ∈ ℝ w × w \bm{M}\in\mathbbm{R}^{w\times w} multiplied by 𝒛 ℓ ∈ ℝ w \bm{z}^{\ell}\in\mathbbm{R}^{w} (We can append with 0 0 s if necessary so that the input from the previous gates can be written as w w -length vector). Thus, we can apply Corollary H.20 to simulate 𝒞 ℓ lin \mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{lin}} with an equivalent ( n , log ⁡ w , d , 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ w ) , d ) − BaseConv \left(n,\log{w},d,\mathcal{O}(\log{w}),d\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} model.

Next, if 𝒞 ℓ mult \mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{mult}} instead consists of only the multiplication gates. Then, we note here that the output 𝒛 ℓ \bm{z}^{\ell} may not exactly equal the input to 𝒞 ℓ mult \mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{mult}} . Nevertheless, we can apply a 𝒪 ⁡ ( w ) \mathcal{O}(w) sparse linear map 𝐑 ∈ ℝ w × w {\bf R}\in\mathbbm{R}^{w\times w} so that 𝐑 ​ 𝒛 ℓ {\bf R}\bm{z}^{\ell} yields vectors 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , \bm{v}^{1},\bm{v}^{2}, and 𝒗 3 \bm{v}^{3} , where 𝒗 1 \bm{v}_{1} constitutes the “first” input to all the multiplication gates and 𝒗 2 \bm{v}_{2} constitutes all the “second” inputs while 𝒗 3 \bm{v}^{3} consists of all entries needed as inputs in the subsequent layers. That is, for the i i th gate in 𝒞 ℓ mult \mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{mult}} , we compute 𝒗 i 1 ⋅ 𝒗 i 2 \bm{v}^{1}_{i}\cdot\bm{v}^{2}_{i} . This implies that for all the gates in 𝒞 ℓ mult \mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{mult}} , we can simply compute 𝒗 1 ⊙ 𝒗 2 \bm{v}_{1}\odot\bm{v}_{2} . To this end, we use the 𝚛𝚎𝚖𝚎𝚖𝚋𝚎𝚛 {\tt remember} primitive with constant number of layers from Proposition H.10 to define a ( n , 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ w ) , d , w , d ) − BaseConv \left(n,\mathcal{O}(\log{w}),d,w,d\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} model that remembers 𝒗 3 \bm{v}^{3} while performing the Hadamard product of 𝒗 1 \bm{v}^{1} with 𝒗 2 \bm{v}^{2} .

Overall, we can then collect all the resulting BaseConv layers and compose them as in Lemma H.11 to simulate 𝒞 \mathcal{C} . Overall, the number of layers used is given by 𝒪 ⁡ ( Δ ​ log ⁡ w ) \mathcal{O}(\Delta\log{w}) while the internal dimension remains fixed at w w . ∎

Using the outline in the proof sketch, we now delve into a detailed analysis of the arithmetic circuit 𝒞 \mathcal{C} to an equivalent BaseConv model.

###### Proof of Theorem H.21 .

Let 𝒖 ∈ ℝ n ​ d \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{nd} be the input to the arithmetic circuit 𝒞 \mathcal{C} with depth Δ \Delta and width w w . We begin by rearranging the circuit into layers of addition and multiplication gates. That is, each layer 𝒞 ℓ \mathcal{C}^{\ell} has either all addition gates or all multiplication gates. This allows us to readily apply the results from Section H.4 . Note here that we can assert that the number of such layers L 𝒞 = 𝒪 ⁡ ( Δ ) L_{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{O}(\Delta) . Moreover, we construe the input to each such circuit as a vector of length w {w} by appending with extra 0s if necessary so that the composition of the layers results in a circuit equivalent to 𝒞 \mathcal{C} . See Fig. 8 for an example of such decomposition

Let 𝒛 ℓ ∈ ℝ w \bm{z}^{\ell}\in\mathbbm{R}^{w} denote the input to each layer 𝒞 ℓ \mathcal{C}_{\ell} for ℓ ∈ [ L 𝒞 ] \ell\in[L_{\mathcal{C}}] with 𝒛 1 ≡ ( 𝒖 , 𝟎 w − n ​ d ) \bm{z}^{1}\equiv(\bm{u},\bm{0}^{w-nd}) . It is important to note here that we may not have 𝒛 ℓ + 1 ≡ 𝒞 ℓ ​ ( 𝒛 ℓ ) \bm{z}^{\ell+1}\equiv\mathcal{C}_{\ell}(\bm{z}^{\ell}) since the inputs for gates at the ( ℓ + 1 ) (\ell+1) -th layer may come from any of the previous layers. We now handle the case of layers with addition gates, multiplication gates, and the intermediate stage separately.

##### Addition Gates.

Let 𝒞 ℓ lin \mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{lin}} denote an arbitrary linear layer which only contains the addition gates that takes in 𝒛 ℓ \bm{z}^{\ell} as input. We know that there exists a matrix 𝐌 ∈ ℝ w × w ′ {\bf M}\in\mathbbm{R}^{w\times w^{\prime}} such that we have 𝒞 ℓ lin ​ ( 𝒛 ℓ ) ≡ 𝐌 ​ 𝒛 ℓ . \mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{lin}}(\bm{z}^{\ell})\equiv{\bf M}\bm{z}^{\ell}. Here, note here that we may need entries from the vector 𝒛 ℓ \bm{z}^{\ell} in subsequent layers. Let s s be the total number of such entries, then for at each index for such entries i ∈ [ w ] i\in[w] , we have the corresponding index i s ∈ [ s ] i_{s}\in[s] . We then append the i i th standard row vector into the matrix 𝐌 {\bf M} to get the matrix 𝐌 ′ ∈ ℝ w × ( w ′ + s ) {\bf M}^{\prime}\in\mathbbm{R}^{w\times(w^{\prime}+s)} so that we have ( 𝐌 ′ ​ 𝒛 ℓ ) ​ [ j ] = { ( 𝐌 ​ 𝒛 ℓ ) ​ [ j ] if ​ j < w ′ 𝒛 ℓ ​ [ i ] if ​ j = w ′ + i s \left({\bf M}^{\prime}\bm{z}^{\ell}\right)[j]=\begin{cases}\left({\bf M}\bm{z}^{\ell}\right)[j]&\text{if }j<w^{\prime}\\ \bm{z}^{\ell}[i]&\text{if }j=w^{\prime}+i_{s}\end{cases} Here, note that we must have w ′ + s ≤ w w^{\prime}+s\leq w as w w is the width of the circuit. If needed, we then append the matrix 𝐌 ′ {\bf M}^{\prime} with all-zero rows 𝟎 w \bm{0}^{w} so that we get the matrix 𝐌 ′′ ∈ ℝ w × w {\bf M}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbbm{R}^{w\times w} . Note here that we have thus preserved all entries needed in subsequent layers and incorporated the output of the ℓ \ell th layer with the output 𝐌 ′′ ​ 𝒛 ℓ ≡ 𝒛 ℓ + 1 {\bf M}^{\prime\prime}\bm{z}^{\ell}\equiv\bm{z}^{\ell+1} serving as the input of the ( ℓ + 1 ) (\ell+1) -th layer. Applying Corollary H.20 then yields an equivalent ( n , 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ w ) , d , w , d ) − BaseConv \left(n,\mathcal{O}(\log{w}),d,w,d\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} model.

##### Multiplication Gates.

Next, we deal with the layer 𝒞 mult ℓ \mathcal{C}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{mult}} of multiplication gates by collecting the two inputs to each multiplication gates as 𝒗 1 ℓ , 𝒗 2 ℓ \bm{v}_{1}^{\ell},\bm{v}_{2}^{\ell} . Note that for the input 𝒛 ℓ ∈ ℝ w \bm{z}^{\ell}\in\mathbbm{R}^{w} from the previous layer, we will again have entries that need to be used in the subsequent layers that we will denote as 𝒗 3 ℓ \bm{v}_{3}^{\ell} . We thus need to permute the entries of 𝒛 ℓ \bm{z}^{\ell} to get the vector [ 𝒗 1 ℓ : : 𝒗 2 ℓ : : 𝒗 3 ℓ ] [\bm{v}_{1}^{\ell}::\bm{v}_{2}^{\ell}::\bm{v}_{3}^{\ell}] so that we can remember 𝒗 3 ℓ \bm{v}_{3}^{\ell} while taking the Hadamard product 𝒗 1 ℓ ⊙ 𝒗 2 ℓ \bm{v}_{1}^{\ell}\odot\bm{v}_{2}^{\ell} . To this end, we can achieve this permutation of entries using a 𝒪 ⁡ ( w ) \mathcal{O}(w) -sparse linear matrix 12 12 12 Each row of 𝐑 ℓ {\bf R}^{\ell} has exactly one non-zero entry. 𝐑 ℓ ∈ ℝ w × w {\bf R}^{\ell}\in\mathbbm{R}^{w\times w} with which is equivalently represented by an ( w , w , 1 , w ) (w,w,1,w) -linear arithmetic circuit that simply moves the appropriate wires from the previous layer. This can again be achieved by a equivalent ( n , 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ w ) , d , w , d ) − BaseConv \left(n,\mathcal{O}(\log{w}),d,w,d\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} model. That is, 𝐑 ℓ ​ 𝒛 ℓ {\bf R}^{\ell}\bm{z}^{\ell} has the following form: 𝐑 ℓ ​ 𝒛 ℓ ≡ ( 𝒗 1 ℓ 𝒗 2 ℓ 𝒗 3 ℓ ) . {\bf R}^{\ell}\bm{z}^{\ell}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{v}_{1}^{\ell}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{v}_{2}^{\ell}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{v}_{3}^{\ell}\end{pmatrix}. Next, we can now define a ( n , 1 , d , n , d ) − BaseConv \left(n,1,d,n,d\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} which extracts 𝒗 1 ℓ \bm{v}_{1}^{\ell} as the projection with the input to the remember primitive given by 𝒚 ℓ ≡ ( 𝒗 2 ℓ , 𝒗 3 ℓ , 𝟎 ) \bm{y}^{\ell}\equiv(\bm{v}_{2}^{\ell},\bm{v}_{3}^{\ell},\bm{0}) . We then specify the remember primitive as 𝚛𝚎𝚖𝚎𝚖𝚋𝚎𝚛 ( 𝒚 ℓ : 𝒗 2 ℓ , 𝒗 3 ℓ , 𝒆 0 , 𝒗 1 ℓ ) {\tt remember}\left(\bm{y}^{\ell}:\bm{v}_{2}^{\ell},\bm{v}_{3}^{\ell},\bm{e}_{0},\bm{v}_{1}^{\ell}\right) which computes the following 𝒛 ℓ + 1 \displaystyle\bm{z}^{\ell+1} ≡ ( 𝒗 1 ℓ ⊙ ( 𝒆 0 ∗ 𝒗 2 ℓ ) 𝒗 3 ℓ 𝟎 ) \displaystyle\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{v}_{1}^{\ell}\odot(\bm{e}_{0}\ast\bm{v}_{2}^{\ell})\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{v}_{3}^{\ell}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}\end{pmatrix} ≡ ( 𝒗 1 ℓ ⊙ 𝒗 2 ℓ 𝒗 3 ℓ 𝟎 ) \displaystyle\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\bm{v}_{1}^{\ell}\odot\bm{v}_{2}^{\ell}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{v}_{3}^{\ell}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{0}\end{pmatrix} ≡ 𝒞 mult ℓ ​ ( 𝒛 ℓ ) ∈ ℝ w . \displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{C}^{\ell}_{\mathrm{mult}}(\bm{z}^{\ell})\in\mathbbm{R}^{w}. Using Proposition H.10 , we know that this requires a ( n , 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ w ) , d , w , d ) − BaseConv \left(n,\mathcal{O}(\log{w}),d,w,d\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} which remembers the entries that will serve as inputs in subsequent layers while performing the Hadamard product 𝒗 1 ⊙ 𝒗 2 \bm{v}^{1}\odot\bm{v}^{2} .

Overall, we can now stack all the resulting BaseConv layers and compose them as in Lemma H.11 to simulate 𝒞 \mathcal{C} . Overall, the number of layers blows up by 𝒪 ⁡ ( Δ ​ log ⁡ w ) \mathcal{O}(\Delta\log{w}) while the internal dimension remains fixed at w w . ∎

(7) The above helps us infer that 𝒛 n \bm{z}^{n} can be expressed as a polynomial in { 𝒖 ⁡ [ 0 , 0 ] , 𝒖 ⁡ [ 0 , 1 ] , … , 𝒖 ⁡ [ N − 1 , d − 1 ] } \{\bm{u}[0,0],\bm{u}[0,1],\ldots,\bm{u}[N-1,d-1]\} . For each polynomial, there exists an arithmetic circuit that computes the polynomial in a natural way, whence we can apply Theorem H.21 to get an equivalent BaseConv model.

###### Corollary H.22 .

For a RetNet model with 𝒪 ⁡ ( d 2 ) \mathcal{O}(d^{2}) parameters and N N layers, there exists an equivalent BaseConv model that uses 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ d ) \mathcal{O}(Nd) parameters and 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ log ⁡ d ) \mathcal{O}(N\log{d}) layers.

We will start by describing the arithmetic circuit that computes the states 𝒛 n ∈ ℝ d × d \bm{z}^{n}\in\mathbbm{R}^{d\times d} here. Indeed, each state requires exactly two alternative layers with only multiplication gates and only addition gates, respectively. First, we note that for each n ∈ N n\in N , we have 𝒛 n ≡ γ 𝒛 n − 1 + ( 𝐀 [ n , : ] ) ⊤ 𝐕 [ n , : ] . \bm{z}^{n}\equiv\gamma\bm{z}^{n-1}+\left({\bf A}[n,:]\right)^{\top}{\bf V}[n,:]. Since we can compute 𝒛 0 \bm{z}^{0} in 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathcal{O}(1) layers and computing γ ⋅ 𝒛 n − 1 \gamma\cdot\bm{z}^{n-1} needs exactly one layer with each entry from 𝒛 n − 1 \bm{z}^{n-1} serving as an input to a multiplication gate along with γ \gamma , the depth of the circuit is 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ) \mathcal{O}(N) with width d 2 d^{2} . We can thus apply Theorem H.21 to get an ( 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ) , 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ log ⁡ d ) , d 2 , 𝒪 ⁡ ( d 2 ) , 𝒪 ⁡ ( d 2 ) ) − (\mathcal{O}(N),\mathcal{O}(N\log{d}),d^{2},\mathcal{O}(d^{2}),\mathcal{O}(d^{2}))- BaseConv model that can compute 𝒛 n \bm{z}^{n} for each n ∈ N n\in N . ∎

##### The Space Complexity of AR

In the context of the Retnet Model and associative recall, it is worth exploring the space complexity of the associative recall (AR) problem: The AR problem takes key-value pairs { 𝒌 i , 𝒗 i } i = 0 n − 1 \{\bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n-1} along with a query 𝒒 \bm{q} appended at the end as input and the goal is to output 𝒗 i \bm{v}_{i} if 𝒒 = 𝒌 i \bm{q}=\bm{k}_{i} for some i ∈ [ 0 , N − 1 ] i\in[0,N-1] . Indeed, we will first provide a lower-bound for any model that purports to solve AR. To this end, we will require a randomized communication complexity lower bound result for the index problem : The index problem has two agents, Alice and Bob, where Alice has a string 𝒙 ∈ { 0 , 1 } n \bm{x}\in\{0,1\}^{n} and Bob has an index i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] , and the goal for the players is to output the i i -th entry 𝒙 i \bm{x}_{i} . Moreover, we also require the communication to be one-way : only Alice is allowed to send a single message to Bob and Bob needs to output the answer. We will make use of the following lower-bound result.

###### Theorem H.23 ( [ Jayram et al., 2008 ] ) .

The one-way randomized communication complexity 13 13 13 The randomized communication complexity of function f f is defined as min π ⁡ ‖ π ‖ \min_{\pi}\left\lVert\pi\right\rVert , where π \pi ranges over all randomized protocols that can solve f f with probability of success at least 2 / 3 2/3 . of the index problem for sending an n n -length bit string is Ω ⁡ ( n ) \Omega(n) .

We now use Theorem H.23 to provide a lower bound on the number of bits required by the Retnet model to solve AR.

###### Corollary H.24 .

The RetNet model requires Ω ⁡ ( N ) \Omega(N) -bits to solve AR for d ≤ N d\leq\sqrt{N} .

###### Proof.

Consider an instance ( 𝒙 , i ) (\bm{x},i) of the index problem with 𝒙 ∈ { 0 , 1 } N \bm{x}\in\{0,1\}^{N} . We now describe the corresponding instance of the AR problem: { i , 𝒙 i } i = 0 N − 1 , i . \{i,\bm{x}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{N-1},i. (8) Next, consider the following one-way protocol for solving the index problem using the RetNet model. Alice with their access of 𝒙 ∈ { 0 , 1 } N \bm{x}\in\{0,1\}^{N} generate an input for AR (without the query) as in equation 8 . Alice then runs the RetNet model on { i , 𝒙 i } i = 0 N − 1 \{i,\bm{x}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{N-1} and sends the memory content of running the RetNet model to Bob. This should include the output 𝒛 N − 1 \bm{z}^{N-1} as we can reasonably assume that both have access weight matrices 𝐖 A , 𝐖 C , 𝐖 V {\bf W}_{A},{\bf W}_{C},{\bf W}_{V} and the scalar γ \gamma . Since we assume that this model solves AR, the output 𝙾𝚞𝚝 [ N , : ] = 𝒙 i {\tt Out}[N,:]=\bm{x}_{i} should contain the associated value of i i . Here, Bob can compute 𝙾𝚞𝚝 [ N , : ] {\tt Out}[N,:] by using the memory content sent by Alice along with the term γ ​ 𝒛 N − 1 \gamma\bm{z}^{N-1} : 𝒙 i = 𝙾𝚞𝚝 [ N , : ] = 𝐂 [ N , : ] 𝒛 N = 𝐂 [ N , : ] ( γ 𝒛 N − 1 + ( 𝐀 [ N , : ] ) ⊤ 𝐕 [ N , : ] ) . \bm{x}_{i}={\tt Out}[N,:]={\bf C}[N,:]\bm{z}^{N}={\bf C}[N,:]\left(\gamma\bm{z}^{N-1}+({\bf A}[N,:])^{\top}{\bf V}[N,:]\right). That is, the total number of bits that are communicated in this protocol is O ⁡ ( d 2 ) O(d^{2}) . For d ≤ N d\leq\sqrt{N} , have shown that a one-way communication protocol exists for solving the index problem exists that uses o ⁡ ( N ) o(N) communication complexity. This contradicts Theorem H.23 and hence, we conclude that the ReNet model solving AR also needs Ω ⁡ ( N ) \Omega(N) bits. ∎

### H.7 The Multiple-Query Associative Recall Problem

#### H.7.1 Introduction

In this section, we consider a general version of the associative recall problem [ Ba et al., 2016 ] .

##### Setup.

Next, we will redefine the multiple-query associative recall problem ( Mqar ) from Definition 3.1 to a slightly more general problem: Suppose we are given an input sequence 𝒖 [ 0 ⋯ N − 1 ] ≜ { ( 𝒌 0 , 𝒗 0 , 𝒒 0 ) , … , ( 𝒌 N 3 − 1 , 𝒗 N 3 − 1 , 𝒒 N 3 − 1 ) } \bm{u}[0\cdots N-1]\triangleq\left\{\left(\bm{k}_{0},\bm{v}_{0},\bm{q}_{0}\right),\ldots,\left(\bm{k}_{\frac{N}{3}-1},\bm{v}_{\frac{N}{3}-1},\bm{q}_{\frac{N}{3}-1}\right)\right\} with each 𝒌 i , 𝒗 i , 𝒒 i ∈ C \bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{i},\bm{q}_{i}\in C is a token drawn from a vocabulary of size c = | C | c=|C| . Our goal is then to check, for each 1 ≤ i ≤ N 3 − 1 1\leq i\leq\frac{N}{3}-1 , whether there exists 0 ≤ j < i 0\leq j<i such that 𝒒 i ≡ 𝒌 j \bm{q}_{i}\equiv\bm{k}_{j} , and if so, output 𝒗 j \bm{v}_{j} . Here, we note that it suffices to have d ≈ log ⁡ ( c ) d\approx\log(c) so that 𝒌 i , 𝒗 i , 𝒒 i \bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{i},\bm{q}_{i} is embedded in { 0 , 1 } d \{0,1\}^{d} . However, we will specify the specific embedding being used for the results below. Here, we construe the tokens 𝒌 i , 𝒒 i \bm{k}_{i},\bm{q}_{i} and 𝒗 i \bm{v}_{i} to be the keys , the queries , and the associated values . Indeed, it might be helpful to think of the input 𝒖 \bm{u} as a streaming sequence of key-value pairs for which we sequentially employ standard associative recall for every key that shows up in the sequence so far.

To see that the above generalizes Definition 3.1 , considers a sequence of length N 3 \frac{N}{3} : 𝒖 [ 0 ⋯ N − 1 ] := { 𝒙 0 , … , 𝒙 N − 1 } \bm{u}[0\cdots N-1]:=\{\bm{x}_{0},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N-1}\} , where each 𝒙 i ∈ C \bm{x}_{i}\in C . The goal of Definition 3.1 is then to check, for each 1 ≤ i < N − 1 1\leq i<N-1 , whether there exists 0 ≤ j < i 0\leq j<i such that 𝒙 i ≡ 𝒙 j \bm{x}_{i}\equiv\bm{x}_{j} , and if so, output 𝒙 j + 1 \bm{x}_{j+1} , and continue otherwise. We can reduce this problem to the above general formulation by taking the following sequence of tuples as the input { ( 𝒙 i , 𝒙 i + 1 , 𝒙 i ) } \{(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{i+1},\bm{x}_{i})\} .

###### Remark H.25 .

As noted above, this version is more general than Definition 3.1 . Thus, the results proven in the sequel, which are proven for the above general Mqar can be ported (with constant blowup in parameters) to get the results corresponding results for Definition 3.1 .

#### H.7.2 Mqar Solution via Attention

Before describing how BaseConv solves the multiple-query associative recall problem, we discuss how Attention solves it trivially using pairwise inner-products. To this end, we will specify how the input is presented to attention.

###### Remark H.26 .

We note that the input for the multiple-query associative recall problem 𝒖 ∈ { 0 , 1 } N × d \bm{u}\in\{0,1\}^{N\times d} has designated indices for the keys, queries, and values in the sequence. We gather these indices below: 𝒦 \displaystyle\mathcal{K} = { i ∈ { 0 , … , N − 1 } | i ≡ 0 mod 3 } , \displaystyle=\{i\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}\lvert\ i\equiv 0\mod{3}\}, (9) 𝒱 \displaystyle\mathcal{V} = { i ∈ { 0 , … , N − 1 } | i ≡ 1 mod 3 } , \displaystyle=\{i\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}\lvert\ i\equiv 1\mod{3}\}, 𝒬 \displaystyle\mathcal{Q} = { i ∈ { 0 , … , N − 1 } | i ≡ 2 mod 3 } , . \displaystyle=\{i\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}\lvert\ i\equiv 2\mod{3}\},. The input 𝒖 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} to Attention for d = 3 ​ c d=3c is then given by 𝒖 [ i , : ] ≡ { [ 𝒌 i : 𝟎 c : 𝟎 c ] if ​ i ∈ 𝒦 [ 𝟎 c : 𝒗 i : 𝟎 c ] if ​ i ∈ 𝒱 [ 𝟎 c : 𝟎 c : 𝒒 i ] if ​ i ∈ 𝒬 \bm{u}[i,:]\equiv\begin{cases}[\bm{k}_{i}:\bm{0}^{c}:\bm{0}^{c}]&\text{ if }i\in\mathcal{K}\\ [\bm{0}^{c}:\bm{v}_{i}:\bm{0}^{c}]&\text{ if }i\in\mathcal{V}\\ [\bm{0}^{c}:\bm{0}^{c}:\bm{q}_{i}]&\text{ if }i\in\mathcal{Q}\end{cases} Here, each 𝒌 i , 𝒗 i , 𝒒 i \bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{i},\bm{q}_{i} is embedded as a one-hot encoding in { 0 , 1 } c \{0,1\}^{c} .

Without softmax, the output for an attention layer 𝐎 ∈ ℝ N × d {\bf O}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} is given by 𝐎 ≡ ( 𝐐𝐊 ⊤ ) ​ 𝐕 , {\bf O}\equiv\left({\bf Q}{\bf K}^{\top}\right){\bf V}, (10) where 𝐐 , 𝐊 , 𝐕 ∈ ℝ N × d {\bf Q},{\bf K},{\bf V}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} are defined as 𝒖 ​ 𝐖 Q , 𝒖 ​ 𝐖 K , 𝒖 ​ 𝐖 V \bm{u}{\bf W}_{Q},\bm{u}{\bf W}_{K},\bm{u}{\bf W}_{V} for 𝒖 ∈ ℝ N × d \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{N\times d} . Instead of position embeddings, we use ALiBi, a popular technique that biases the attention scores 𝐐𝐊 ⊤ {\bf Q}{\bf K}^{\top} with a lower-triangular Toeplitz matrix 𝐁 ∈ ℝ N × N {\bf B}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N} [ Press et al., 2021 ] . The values in this matrix are controlled by a fixed hyperparameter so they do not count towards the number of parameters in the model.

###### Proposition H.27 .

Given an input 𝐮 ∈ { 0 , 1 } N × d \bm{u}\in\{0,1\}^{N\times d} (encoded as in Remark H.26 ) where d = 3 ​ c d=3c , Attention with linear biases (even without using soft-max) solves Mqar for 𝐮 \bm{u} using 𝒪 ⁡ ( c 2 ) {\mathcal{O}}(c^{2}) parameters, 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ c 2 + N 2 ​ c ) {\mathcal{O}}(Nc^{2}+N^{2}c) time complexity and 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) {\mathcal{O}}(1) layers.

###### Proof.

We use two layers of attention. We will start by specifying the projection matrices for the first layer 𝐖 Q 1 , 𝐖 K 1 , 𝐖 V 1 ∈ ℝ d × d {\bf W}_{Q}^{1},{\bf W}^{1}_{K},{\bf W}^{1}_{V}\in\mathbbm{R}^{d\times d} as: 𝐖 K 1 ≡ 𝐖 Q 1 ≡ 𝟎 , 𝐖 V 1 ≡ ( 𝟎 𝟎 𝟎 𝟎 𝐈 𝐜 × 𝐜 𝟎 𝟎 𝟎 𝟎 ) {\bf W}^{1}_{K}\equiv{\bf W}^{1}_{Q}\equiv{\bf 0},\quad{\bf W}_{V}^{1}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}{\bf 0}&{\bf 0}&{\bf 0}\\ {\bf 0}&{\bf I_{c\times c}}&{\bf 0}\\ {\bf 0}&{\bf 0}&{\bf 0}\end{pmatrix}

Above, 𝐖 𝐕 𝟏 {\bf W_{V}^{1}} is meant to isolate the 𝒗 i \bm{v}_{i} embeddings. For the first layer, we then have 𝐐 1 ≡ 𝐊 1 ≡ 𝟎 {\bf Q}^{1}\equiv{\bf K}^{1}\equiv{\bf 0} , and 𝐕 1 [ i , : ] := 𝒖 1 [ i , : ] 𝐖 V 1 ≡ { [ 𝟎 c : 𝒗 i : 𝟎 c ] if ​ i ∈ 𝒱 , 𝟎 d otherwise , {\bf V}^{1}[i,:]:=\bm{u}^{1}[i,:]{\bf W}_{V}^{1}\equiv\begin{cases}[\bm{0}^{c}:\bm{v}_{i}:\bm{0}^{c}]&\text{if }i\in\mathcal{V},\\ {\bf 0}^{d}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}, where 𝒦 , 𝒬 , 𝒱 \mathcal{K},\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{V} are defined as in equation 9 . Here, note that the values have been shifted to the corresponding key position. \displaystyle{\bf O}^{2}[i,:] = ( ( 𝐐 2 𝐊 2 ⊤ + 𝐁 2 ) 𝐕 2 ) [ i , : ] \displaystyle=\left(\left({\bf Q}^{2}{\bf K}^{2\top}+{{\bf B}^{2}}\right){\bf V}^{2}\right)[i,:] = ( ( 𝐐 2 𝐊 2 ⊤ + 𝟎 ) 𝐕 2 ) [ i , V}^{2} = ∑ j = 0 N − 1 ( 𝐐 2 𝐊 2 ⊤ ) [ i , j ] ⋅ 𝐕 2 [ j , : ] \displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\left({\bf Q}^{2}{\bf K}^{2\top}\right)[i,j]\cdot{\bf V}^{2}[j,:] = ∑ j ∈ 𝒦 ( 𝐐 2 𝐊 2 ⊤ ) [ i , j ] ⋅ [ 𝒗 j \displaystyle=\begin{cases}[\bm{v}_{j}:\bm{0}^{2c}]&\text{if }j\in\mathcal{K},i\in\mathcal{Q},\bm{q}_{i}\equiv\bm{k}_{j}\\ \bm{0}^{d}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}, where we use the fact that for each index j ∈ 𝒦 j\in\mathcal{K} , the matrix 𝐕 2 {\bf V}^{2} contains the associated value from equation 11 . Thus, for each query 𝒒 i \bm{q}_{i} , we solve the associated value problem yielding a match for the j j th key.

In total, we only need 𝒪 ⁡ ( c 2 ) \mathcal{O}(c^{2}) -many parameters to perform these multiplications and the linear bias in the first layer is a hyperparameter that is static and unlearned; the time complexity comes from the multiplication of 𝐐𝐊 ⊤ {\bf QK^{\top}} in 𝒪 ⁡ ( N 2 ​ c ) {\mathcal{O}}(N^{2}c) , and projections in 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ c 2 ) {\mathcal{O}}(Nc^{2}) . Finally, we only need 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathcal{O}(1) layers for this solution. ∎

In the sequel, we develop a parallel algorithm to solve the multiple-query associative recall problem with 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ d ⋅ log 2 ⁡ N ) \mathcal{O}(Nd\cdot\log^{2}{N}) work complexity and 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ⋅ log 2 ⁡ N ) \mathcal{O}(d\cdot\log^{2}{N}) time. We then convert the algorithm into a BaseConv model via the route of arithmetic circuits, which then solves the multiple-query associative recall problem with 𝒪 ~ ​ ( 1 ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1) layers and 𝒪 ~ ​ ( N ​ d ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(Nd) parameters.

#### H.7.3 Initial Attempt: A Sequential Algorithm

We will first discuss the algorithm that simply uses an associative array to solve the multiple-query associative recall problem. Specifically, we want to use a data structure that allows for logarithmic insertion and membership query. Here, we do not specify a choice but data structures including self-balancing binary search trees which allow for 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ N ⋅ d ) \mathcal{O}(\log{N}\cdot d) 𝚒𝚗𝚜𝚎𝚛𝚝 {\tt insert} and 𝚏𝚒𝚗𝚍 {\tt find} operations for d d -bit entries should be sufficient.

###### Proposition H.28 .

Algorithm 9 solves the multiple-query associative recall problem ( Mqar ) \left(\textsc{Mqar}\right) in 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ​ N ​ log ⁡ N ) \mathcal{O}(dN\log{N}) time for an input sequence 𝐮 ∈ { 0 , 1 } N × d \bm{u}\in\{0,1\}^{N\times d} .

###### Proof.

For any i ∈ { 0 , … , N 3 − 1 } i\in\{0,\ldots,\frac{N}{3}-1\} , we know that both insertion and lookup operations take 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( i ) ⋅ d ) \mathcal{O}(\log(i)\cdot d) time. Overall, the runtime of the algorithm is ∑ i = 0 N 3 − 1 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( i ) ⋅ d ) = 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( N ! ) ⋅ d ) = 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ log ​ N ⋅ d ) . \sum_{i=0}^{\frac{N}{3}-1}\mathcal{O}(\log(i)\cdot d)=\mathcal{O}(\log(N!)\cdot d)=\mathcal{O}(N\log{N}\cdot d). ∎

#### H.7.4 Algorithm via Parallel Binary Search

Our plan is to convert the algorithm for solving the multiple-query associative recall problem in the RAM model into an arithmetic circuit, which by Theorem H.21 will lead to a BaseConv model that solves the multiple-query associative recall problem. With respect to Algorithm 9 , it may be the case that the arithmetic circuit has a large number of layers Ω ⁡ ( N ) \Omega(N) . Unfortunately, this would imply that the resulting BaseConv model may have near quadratic complexity in N N . Instead, we now initiate our effort into designing a BaseConv model with both small enough number of parameters and number of layers. Here, we will first subdivide the problem using dyadic intervals into 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ) \mathcal{O}(N) subproblems and reduce each such subproblem into a multiple search problem [ Akl and Meijer, 1990 ] . To this end, we briefly introduce the multiple search problem below. Given two array of numbers A ≜ a 0 ≤ … ≤ a n − 1 A\triangleq a_{0}\leq\ldots\leq a_{n-1} and B ≜ ( b 0 ≤ … ≤ b m − 1 ) B\triangleq(b_{0}\leq\ldots\leq b_{m-1}) with n ≤ m n\leq m , for each a j ∈ A a_{j}\in A , the goal is to find the smallest element in B B that is larger than or equal to a j a_{j} . The multiple search problem is solved by a parallel binary search ( pbs ) algorithm in [ Akl and Meijer, 1990 ] with work complexity 𝒪 ⁡ ( n ⋅ log ⁡ m ) \mathcal{O}(n\cdot\log{m}) and time 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ​ log ⁡ m ) \mathcal{O}(\log{n}\log{m}) . Specifically, for sorted arrays A [ 0 ⋯ n − 1 ] A[0\cdots n-1] and B [ 0 ⋯ m − 1 ] B[0\cdots m-1] , 𝚙𝚋𝚜 {\tt pbs} constructs the array C [ 0 ⋯ n − 1 ] C[0\cdots n-1] defined as C ⁡ [ i ] ≜ { min 0 ≤ j < m { j | A [ i ] ≤ B [ j ] } if ​ A ​ [ i ] ≤ B ⁡ [ m − 1 ] m otherwise. C[i]\triangleq\begin{cases}\min_{0\leq j<m}\{j\lvert\ A[i]\leq B[j]\}&\text{if }A[i]\leq B[m-1]\\ m&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases} (12) The algorithm itself runs in exclusive-read exclusive-write (EREW) PRAM model—no two processors are allowed to read from or write into the same memory location at the same time.

We now augment the algorithm copied from [ Akl and Meijer, 1990 ] for our purposes below.

Let Σ \Sigma be the set { 0 , 1 } \{0,1\} and denote the set of binary strings of size n n as Σ n \Sigma^{n} . We define prefix ⁡ ( 𝒙 ) \mathrm{prefix}(\bm{x}) for n n -bit strings as the set of all initial substrings of 𝒙 ∈ Σ n \bm{x}\in\Sigma^{n} which includes the empty string and 𝒙 \bm{x} itself. Next, let dec : { 0 , 1 } n → ℕ \mathrm{dec}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbbm{N} be the decimal representation of an n n -bit string 𝒙 \bm{x} with 𝒙 ⁡ [ 0 ] \bm{x}[0] denoting the least significant bit. We also use 𝚜𝚘𝚛𝚝 ⁡ ( A ) {\tt sort}(A) as a procedure that sorts an array A A . Finally, wlog, we assume that N N is a power of 2 2 . We are now ready to present a parallel algorithm that solves the multiple-query associative recall problem below.

###### Remark H.29 .

In lines 8 and 11 , we keep track of the sorted permutation of the indices of keys and queries, respectively. This helps us in the retrieval of the index of the matching key as in line 17 .

#### H.7.5 Correctness and Complexity

###### Proposition H.30 .

Algorithm 11 solves the multiple-query associative recall problem with work complexity 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ d ⋅ log 2 ⁡ N ) \mathcal{O}(Nd\cdot\log^{2}{N}) and time 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ⋅ log 2 ⁡ N ) \mathcal{O}(d\cdot\log^{2}{N}) .

###### Proof.

The correctness of pbs implies the correctness of Algorithm 11 if we can show that, for each 1 ≤ i < N 3 1\leq i<\frac{N}{3} , we check for a match among the keys { 𝒌 j } j ∈ [ i − 1 ] \{\bm{k}_{j}\}_{j\in[i-1]} . To this end, for each 1 ≤ i < N 3 1\leq i<\frac{N}{3} , let the set of all iterator indices associated with an index i i be defined as K i ≜ { ( k , x ) | i ∈ J k 𝒙 } K_{i}\triangleq\{(k,x)\lvert i\in J_{k}^{\bm{x}}\} with J k 𝒙 J_{k}^{\bm{x}} as noted in line 10 . Then, we define the corresponding set for keys as ℐ i ≜ ⋃ ( k , 𝒙 ) ∈ K i I k 𝒙 ​ with I k 𝒙 s defined as in line 7 . \mathcal{I}_{i}\triangleq\bigcup_{(k,\bm{x})\in K_{i}}I_{k}^{\bm{x}}\text{ with $I_{k}^{\bm{x}}$s defined as in line \ref{step: i-def}}. That is, for all the calls to pbs-key-values that i i is part of (given by K i K_{i} ) where the algorithm checks for a match among the keys in ℐ i \mathcal{I}_{i} , it then suffices to show that ℐ i = [ i − 1 ] \mathcal{I}_{i}=[i-1] .

Here, first note that if some index j ∈ I k 𝒙 ⊆ ℐ i j\in I_{k}^{\bm{x}}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{i} for some 𝒙 ∈ Σ log ⁡ ( N 3 ) − k \bm{x}\in\Sigma^{\log\left(\frac{N}{3}\right)-k} , then, by definition, 𝒙 ∈ prefix ⁡ ( bin ⁡ ( j ) ) \bm{x}\in\mathrm{prefix}(\mathrm{bin}(j)) . Here, let 𝒙 1 := 𝒙 | 𝒙 ⁡ [ log ⁡ ( N 3 ) − k ] = 1 \bm{x}^{1}:=\bm{x}\lvert_{\bm{x}[\log\left(\frac{N}{3}\right)-k]=1} where we set the ( log ⁡ ( N 3 ) − k ) (\log\left(\frac{N}{3}\right)-k) -th index of 𝒙 \bm{x} to be 1. Consequently, as we have i ∈ J k 𝒙 i\in J^{\bm{x}}_{k} for the same k k and 𝒙 \bm{x} as in I k 𝒙 I_{k}^{\bm{x}} ( cf. line 7 ), we must have 𝒙 1 ∈ prefix ⁡ ( bin ⁡ ( i ) ) \bm{x}^{1}\in\mathrm{prefix}(\mathrm{bin(i)}) . Thus we get j < i j<i , whence we can claim that ℐ i ⊆ [ i − 1 ] \mathcal{I}_{i}\subseteq[i-1] .

For the other direction, for any i i , let b b denote the position of the most significant bit in bin ⁡ ( i ) \mathrm{bin}(i) which differs from bin ⁡ ( j ) \mathrm{bin}(j) for any j ∈ [ i − 1 ] j\in[i-1] . Then, there must exist a binary string that is in the prefix set of both bin ⁡ ( i ) \mathrm{bin}(i) and bin ⁡ ( i ) \mathrm{bin}(i) . That is, there exists 𝒙 ∈ prefix ⁡ ( bin ⁡ ( i ) ) ∩ prefix ⁡ ( bin ⁡ ( j ) ) \bm{x}\in\mathrm{prefix}(\mathrm{bin}(i))\cap\mathrm{prefix}(\mathrm{bin}(j)) with 𝒙 ∈ Σ b \bm{x}\in\Sigma^{b} . Thus, we then must have bin ⁡ ( j ) ∈ I log ⁡ ( N 3 ) − b 𝒙 \mathrm{bin}(j)\in I^{\bm{x}}_{\log\left(\frac{N}{3}\right)-b} and bin ⁡ ( i ) ∈ J log ⁡ ( N 3 ) − b 𝒙 \mathrm{bin}(i)\in J^{\bm{x}}_{\log\left(\frac{N}{3}\right)-b} with 𝒙 \bm{x} as the corresponding witness. Hence, we have [ i − 1 ] ⊆ ℐ i [i-1]\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{i} .

Overall, we have shown that ℐ i = [ i − 1 ] \mathcal{I}_{i}=[i-1] . Since this holds for all 1 ≤ i < N 3 1\leq i<\frac{N}{3} , we can conclude that Algorithm 11 solves the multiple-query associative recall problem.

Next, it is easy to see that we execute lines 7 to 17 ∑ k = 0 log ⁡ ( N 3 ) − 1 N 3 2 k + 1 \sum_{k=0}^{\log\left(\frac{N}{3}\right)-1}\frac{\frac{N}{3}}{2^{k+1}} -many times. We note that sorting n n values each of size d d can be done with work complexity n ​ log ⁡ n ⋅ d n\log{n}\cdot d . We note that, at each instance, we are sorting sorting 2 k 2^{k} values. Meanwhile, remembering 2 k 2^{k} sorted permutation of indices can be done in linear time using arrays. Moreover, each call to pbs-key-values has n = m = 2 k n=m=2^{k} which has work complexity n ​ log ⁡ m n\log{m} . Finally, we know that the work complexity of lines 19 to 21 is 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ) \mathcal{O}(N) . Thus, the overall work complexity of Algorithm 11 is d ⋅ ∑ k = 0 log ⁡ ( N 3 ) − 1 N 3 2 k + 1 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( 2 k ⋅ log ⁡ 2 k ) = d ⋅ 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ) ⋅ ∑ k = 0 log ⁡ N / 3 − 1 𝒪 ⁡ ( k ) = 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ d ⋅ log 2 ⁡ N ) . d\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{\log\left(\frac{N}{3}\right)-1}\frac{\frac{N}{3}}{2^{k+1}}\mathcal{O}(2^{k}\cdot\log{2^{k}})=d\cdot\mathcal{O}(N)\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{\log{N/3}-1}\mathcal{O}(k)=\mathcal{O}(Nd\cdot\log^{2}{N}). (13)

We will now analyze the depth of Algorithm 11 . We know that the depth of computation for Algorithm 10 is 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ​ log ⁡ m ) \mathcal{O}(\log{n}\log{m}) for input sizes. Moreover, we have 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathcal{O}(1) depth for the computation in 19 to 21 as each entry in 𝚒𝚍𝚡 {\tt idx} can have at most one entry. Since the nested for loops iterating over k k s and the associated 𝒙 \bm{x} s runs in parallel, the depth of Algorithm 11 is dominated by the largest depth among all calls to pbs-key-values and to sort . The largest such call to pbs-key-values is of size n = m = 2 log ⁡ ( N 3 ) − 1 = N / 6 n=m=2^{\log\left(\frac{N}{3}\right)-1}=N/6 which yields a depth of d ⋅ log 2 ⁡ N 3 d\cdot\log^{2}{\frac{N}{3}} . Moreover, using sorting networks ( Definition H.34 ), we know that the largest depth is for sorting N 6 \frac{N}{6} values of size d d given by d ⋅ Θ ⁡ ( log ⁡ N ) d\cdot\Theta(\log{N}) ( Lemma H.35 ). Thus, we can conclude that Algorithm 11 takes 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ⋅ log 2 ⁡ N ) \mathcal{O}(d\cdot\log^{2}{N}) , time where N N is the length of the input. ∎

#### H.7.6 Conversion to Arithmetic Circuit

We will convert Algorithm 11 to an arithmetic circuits modularly. In particular, after writing out an explicit circuit for Algorithm 10 , we will uses this circuit as a black-box along with circuits for sorting networks.

##### Circuit for pbs-key-values .

We will denote the corresponding arithmetic circuit for Algorithm 10 as pbs-key-values as well with the input gates comprising of each entry from 𝒒 [ s ⋯ t ] \bm{q}[s\cdots t] and 𝒌 [ x ⋯ y ] \bm{k}[x\cdots y] and the i i -th output gate yielding the value C ⁡ [ i ] C[i] as in Algorithm 10 .

Here, we first convert the comparisons for the if statements in Algorithm 10 . To this end, we briefly introduce comparators.

###### Definition H.31 (Comparators) .

A comparator is a device with inputs 𝒙 \bm{x} and 𝒚 \bm{y} and outputs 𝒙 ′ \bm{x}^{\prime} and 𝒚 ′ \bm{y}^{\prime} , that performs the following function: 𝒙 ′ \displaystyle\bm{x}^{\prime} = min ⁡ ( 𝒙 , 𝒚 ) , \displaystyle=\min(\bm{x},\bm{y}), 𝒚 ′ \displaystyle\bm{y}^{\prime} = max ⁡ ( 𝒙 , 𝒚 ) . \displaystyle=\max(\bm{x},\bm{y}).

Using comparators, we can use bit-wise XOR and AND to define the result of the comparisons in lines 6 and 11 as the following fixed variables: ℓ x \displaystyle\ell_{x} : = 𝟙 { 𝒒 [ mid ] ≤ 𝒌 x } , \displaystyle:=\mathbbm{1}\{\bm{q}[\mathrm{mid}]\leq\bm{k}_{x}\}, (14) g y \displaystyle g_{y} : = 𝟙 { 𝒒 [ mid ] > 𝒌 y } , \displaystyle:=\mathbbm{1}\{\bm{q}[\mathrm{mid}]>\bm{k}_{y}\}, z \displaystyle z : = bin-search ​ ( { 𝒌 i } i = s t , 𝒒 ⁡ [ mid ] ) . \displaystyle:=\texttt{bin-search}({\{\bm{k}_{i}\}_{i=s}^{t},\ \bm{q}[\mathrm{mid}]}). This then allows us to infer the index of the key array for each recursive call to pbs-key-values in lines 9 , 14 , 19 , and 20 from Algorithm 10 . Specifically, let z s z_{s} and z t − 1 z_{t}-1 denote the starting and ending indices for the keys as inputs to the recursive calls in Algorithm 10 ) below: pbs-key-values ( 𝒒 [ mid + 1 ⋯ t ] , 𝒌 [ z t ⋯ y ] ) ; (lines 9 and 20 ) \displaystyle\texttt{pbs-key-values}(\displaystyle\bm{q}[\mathrm{mid}+1\cdots t],\ \bm{k}[z_{t}\cdots y]);\text{ (lines \ref{step: rec-1} and \ref{step: right})} (15) pbs-key-values ( 𝒒 [ s ⋯ mid − 1 ] , 𝒌 [ x ⋯ z s − 1 ] ) ; (lines 14 and 19 ) \displaystyle\texttt{pbs-key-values}(\bm{q}[s\cdots\mathrm{mid}-1],\ \bm{k}[x\ \cdots z_{s}-1]);\text{ (lines \ref{step: rec-2} and \ref{step: left})} (16) Here, z t z_{t} and z s z_{s} can assume values dependent on the results of the comparisons in lines 6 and 11 . Specifically, we have z t \displaystyle z_{t} = ℓ x ⋅ x + ( 1 − ℓ x ) ​ ( 1 − g y ) ⋅ z = { x if ​ 𝒒 ​ [ mid ] ≤ 𝒌 x ​ (line 9 ) z if ​ 𝒒 ​ [ mid ] ∈ ( 𝒌 x , 𝒌 y ] ​ (line 20 ) 0 otherwise , \displaystyle=\ell_{x}\cdot x+(1-\ell_{x})(1-g_{y})\cdot z=\begin{cases}x&\text{if }\bm{q}[\mathrm{mid}]\leq\bm{k}_{x}\text{ (line \ref{step: rec-1})}\\ z&\text{if }\bm{q}[\mathrm{mid}]\in(\bm{k}_{x},\bm{k}_{y}]\text{ (line \ref{step: right})}\\ 0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}, z s \displaystyle z_{s} = g y ⋅ ( y + 1 ) + ( 1 − ℓ x ) ​ ( 1 − g y ) ⋅ z = { y + 1 if ​ 𝒒 ​ [ mid ] > 𝒌 y ​ (line 14 ) z if ​ 𝒒 ​ [ mid ] ∈ ( 𝒌 x , 𝒌 y ] ​ (line 19 ) 0 otherwise . \displaystyle=g_{y}\cdot(y+1)+(1-\ell_{x})(1-g_{y})\cdot z=\begin{cases}y+1&\text{if }\bm{q}[\mathrm{mid}]>\bm{k}_{y}\text{ (line \ref{step: rec-2})}\\ z&\text{if }\bm{q}[\mathrm{mid}]\in(\bm{k}_{x},\bm{k}_{y}]\text{ (line \ref{step: left})}\\ 0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}. Here, z s z_{s} or z t z_{t} getting a value 0 0 signifies that the branch is dead, and we do not execute the recursive call.

Finally, let the arrays C t [ mid + 1 ⋯ t ] C_{t}[\mathrm{mid}+1\cdots t] and C s [ s ⋯ mid − 1 ] C_{s}[s\cdots\mathrm{mid}-1] denote the outputs to the recursive calls in equation 15 and equation 16 , respectively. We can then succinctly express the outputs for each index of the output array C C as C ⁡ [ i ] = { ℓ x ⋅ x + z s ⋅ ( C 1 ​ [ i ] ) i ∈ [ s ⋯ mid − 1 ] g y ⋅ ( y + 1 ) + z t ⋅ ( C 2 ​ [ i ] ) i ∈ [ mid + 1 ⋯ t ] ℓ x ⋅ x + ( 1 − ℓ x ) ​ ( 1 − g y ) ⋅ z + g y ⋅ ( y + 1 ) i = mid C[i]=\begin{cases}\ell_{x}\cdot x+z_{s}\cdot(C_{1}[i])&i\in[s\cdots\mathrm{mid}-1]\\ g_{y}\cdot(y+1)+z_{t}\cdot(C_{2}[i])&i\in[\mathrm{mid}+1\cdots t]\\ \ell_{x}\cdot x+(1-\ell_{x})(1-g_{y})\cdot z+g_{y}\cdot(y+1)&i=\mathrm{mid}\\ \end{cases} (17)

We can thus state the circuit schematically in Fig. 9 .

Now, before accounting for the complexity of the circuit for Algorithm 10 , we must first assert the complexity of the comparators that we use in Fig. 9 .

###### Lemma H.32 ( Cormen et al. [2022] ) .

For binary strings 𝐱 , 𝐲 ∈ Σ d \bm{x},\bm{y}\in\Sigma^{d} of length d d , there exists a comparison network of size 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ) \mathcal{O}(d) , width 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ) \mathcal{O}(d) , and depth 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ) \mathcal{O}(\log{d}) .

We use this lemma to compute the complexity of the arhmetic circuit from Fig. 9 .

###### Proposition H.33 .

There exists an ( 𝒪 ⁡ ( ( n + m ) ​ d ) , 𝒪 ⁡ ( n ​ d ⋅ ( log ⁡ m + log ⁡ n ) ) , 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ⁡ ( log ⁡ m + log ⁡ n ​ log ​ d ) ) , 𝒪 ⁡ ( n ​ d ) ) (\mathcal{O}((n+m)d),\mathcal{O}(nd\cdot(\log{m}+\log{n})),\mathcal{O}(\log{n}(\log{m}+\log{n}\log{d})),\mathcal{O}(nd)) -arithmetic circuit 14 14 14 Recall that a ( n , s , Δ , w ) (n,s,\Delta,w) -arithmetic circuit is an n n -variate circuit with size s s , depth at most Δ \Delta , and width w w . equivalent to pbs-key-values ( Algorithm 10 ) with inputs 𝐪 \bm{q} and 𝐤 \bm{k} of lengths n n and m m with d d -bit entries.

###### Proof.

The size of the circuit for pbs-key-values should equal the work-complexity of Algorithm 10 but we also need to account for the comparison gates in equation 14 . Further, the circuit for binary search also has a size of 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ⋅ n ) \mathcal{O}(d\cdot n) instead of 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ⋅ log ⁡ n ) \mathcal{O}(d\cdot\log{n}) work as in Algorithm 10 . Using Lemma H.32 , along with the fact that the comparison gates and the binary search are used at most 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) \mathcal{O}(\log{n}) times, we deduce that we are adding 𝒪 ⁡ ( n ​ d ​ log ⁡ n ) \mathcal{O}(nd\log{n}) size to the circuit in addition to the work complexity of the parallel algorithm. Thus, the overall size of the arithmetic circuit for pbs-key-values is 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ​ n ​ log ⁡ m + n ​ d ​ log ⁡ n ) \mathcal{O}(dn\log{m}+nd\log{n}) . Further, the depth of the circuit here is determined by the runtime of Algorithm 10 along with the depth of the comparison gates and binary search 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ​ log ⁡ d ) \mathcal{O}(\log{n}\log{d}) , and finally, at most n n processors are used for the parallel algorithm in Algorithm 10 , which yields the width of the circuit. ∎

##### Circuit for Parallel-MQAR .

Now, we will call the circuit for Parallel-MQAR with the same name while the input gates contain the inputs of Algorithm 11 . Indeed, we can directly “translate” Algorithm 11 to an arithmetic circuit as the values for I k 𝒙 I_{k}^{\bm{x}} and I k 𝒙 I_{k}^{\bm{x}} for each 𝒙 \bm{x} and k k are predetermined from N N . Thus, we start by placing the corresponding sorting networks which feeds into the pbs-key-values ( 𝒒 [ s ⋯ t ] , 𝒌 [ x ⋯ y ] , n , m ) \texttt{pbs-key-values}\left(\bm{q}[s\cdots t],\ \bm{k}[x\cdots y],n,m\right) circuit for Algorithm 10 in Fig. 9 so that the output values from the calls to pbs-key-values result in the checks as in line 17 of Algorithm 11 . That is, we get outputs C k ​ [ J permuted k ​ 𝒙 ​ ( dec ⁡ ( i ) ) ] C_{k}[J_{\mathrm{permuted}}^{k\bm{x}}(\mathrm{dec}(i))] from each call to pbs-key-values . We can then use a comparison gate to check if this value equals 2 k 2^{k} , and if not, we have found a match C k ​ [ J permuted k ​ 𝒙 ​ ( dec ⁡ ( i ) ) ] C_{k}[J_{\mathrm{permuted}}^{k\bm{x}}(\mathrm{dec}(i))] for the query 𝒒 i \bm{q}_{i} which results in the output of the associated value 𝒗 C k ​ [ J permuted k ​ 𝒙 ​ ( dec ⁡ ( i ) ) ] + 1 \bm{v}_{C_{k}[J_{\mathrm{permuted}}^{k\bm{x}}(\mathrm{dec}(i))]+1} , exactly as in Algorithm 11 . That is, we first define the following variable as the output of the comparison gate: 𝒄 dec ⁡ ( i ) k := 𝟙 { C k [ J permuted k ​ 𝒙 ( dec ( i ) ) ] ≠ 2 k } . \bm{c}_{\mathrm{dec}(i)}^{k}:=\mathbbm{1}\{C_{k}[J_{\mathrm{permuted}}^{k\bm{x}}(\mathrm{dec}(i))]\neq 2^{k}\}. (18) Here, as C k ​ [ J permuted k ​ 𝒙 ​ ( dec ⁡ ( i ) ) ] ≠ 2 k C_{k}[J_{\mathrm{permuted}}^{k\bm{x}}(\mathrm{dec}(i))]\neq 2^{k} implies that I permuted k ​ 𝒙 ​ ( C k ​ [ J permuted k ​ 𝒙 ​ ( dec ⁡ ( i ) ) ] ) I_{\mathrm{permuted}}^{k\bm{x}}\left(C_{k}[J_{\mathrm{permuted}}^{k\bm{x}}(\mathrm{dec}(i))]\right) equals the index of the matching key 𝒌 j \bm{k}_{j} corresponding to the query 𝒒 i \bm{q}_{i} , the i i th output is then simply given by 𝒄 dec ⁡ ( i ) k ⋅ I permuted k ​ 𝒙 ​ ( C k ​ [ J permuted k ​ 𝒙 ​ ( dec ⁡ ( i ) ) ] ) \bm{c}_{\mathrm{dec}(i)}^{k}\cdot I_{\mathrm{permuted}}^{k\bm{x}}\left(C_{k}[J_{\mathrm{permuted}}^{k\bm{x}}(\mathrm{dec}(i))]\right) , where the 0 0 output implies that there does not exist a matching key.

Here, we also briefly introduce the the sorting networks that we use to sort the keys and queries:

###### Definition H.34 (Informal) .

Sorting networks are circuits with gates and wires where the gates of the circuit are comparators ( Definition H.31 ) connecting two wires. Each such circuit can perform sorting on a fixed number of values.

We can then show the circuit schematically as in Fig. 10 .

We now dilineate the complexity of the circuit, starting with the complexity of the sorting networks.

###### Lemma H.35 ( Ajtai et al. [1983] ) .

Let A A be an array with d d -bit entries of size n n . Then, one can implement a sorting network to sort the array A A with size 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ⋅ n ​ log ⁡ n ) \mathcal{O}(d\cdot n\log{n}) and depth 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ​ log ⁡ n ) \mathcal{O}(\log{d}\log{n}) .

###### Proposition H.36 .

There exists an ( N ⋅ d , 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ d ⋅ log 2 ⁡ N ) , 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ​ log 2 ​ N ) , 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ d ​ log ​ N ) ) (N\cdot d,\mathcal{O}(Nd\cdot\log^{2}{N}),\mathcal{O}(\log{d}\log^{2}{N}),\mathcal{O}(Nd\log{N})) -arithmetic circuit that solves the multiple-query associative recall problem.

###### Proof.

We note here that for each k k , there are N 3 2 k + 1 \frac{\frac{N}{3}}{2^{k+1}} parallel problems of size 2 k 2^{k} for both the sorting networks and the 𝚙𝚋𝚜 − 𝚔𝚎𝚢 − 𝚟𝚊𝚕𝚞𝚎𝚜 {\tt pbs-key-values} circuit. Using Lemma H.35 , the cumulative size of these sorting networks is 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ⋅ N ​ log 2 ​ N ) \mathcal{O}(d\cdot N\log^{2}{N}) (see equation 13 ) with overall depth 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ​ log ⁡ N ) \mathcal{O}(\log{d}\log{N}) .

Similarly, the next layer again runs ∑ k = 0 log ⁡ N 3 − 1 N 3 2 k + 1 \sum_{k=0}^{\log{\frac{N}{3}}-1}\frac{\frac{N}{3}}{2^{k+1}} -many circuits for 𝚙𝚋𝚜 − 𝚔𝚎𝚢 − 𝚟𝚊𝚕𝚞𝚎𝚜 {\tt pbs-key-values} each of which has size 𝒪 ⁡ ( 2 k ​ d ​ ( log ⁡ 2 k + log ⁡ 2 k ) ) = O ⁡ ( d ⋅ 2 k ​ log ⁡ 2 k ) \mathcal{O}(2^{k}d(\log{2^{k}}+\log{2^{k}}))=O(d\cdot 2^{k}\log{2^{k}}) , depth 𝒪 ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ 2 k ​ log ​ d ) \mathcal{O}(\log^{2}{2^{k}}\log{d}) and width 𝒪 ⁡ ( 2 k ) \mathcal{O}(2^{k}) ( Proposition H.33 ). Again, the cumulative size of this layer is given by 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ d ⋅ log 2 ⁡ N ) \mathcal{O}(Nd\cdot\log^{2}{N}) (see equation 13 ). Since we run each of these circuits in parallel, the depth of this layer is again 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ​ log 2 ⁡ ( N ) ) \mathcal{O}(\log{d}\log^{2}(N)) while the width is 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ⋅ log ⁡ N ) \mathcal{O}(N\cdot\log{N}) .

Finally, we perform N 3 ​ log ⁡ N 3 \frac{N}{3}\log{\frac{N}{3}} comparisons at the end of d d -bit strings in parallel which results in size 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ log ⁡ N ⋅ d ) \mathcal{O}(N\log{N}\cdot d) , depth 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ) \mathcal{O}(\log{d}) and width 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ log ⁡ N ⋅ d ) \mathcal{O}(N\log{N}\cdot d) ( Lemma H.32 ). Therefore, the resulting arithmetic circuit has size 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ⋅ N ​ log 2 ​ N + N ​ d ⋅ log 2 ⁡ N + N ​ log ⁡ N ⋅ d ) = O ⁡ ( N ​ d ​ log 2 ​ N ) \mathcal{O}(d\cdot N\log^{2}{N}+Nd\cdot\log^{2}{N}+N\log{N}\cdot d)=O(Nd\log^{2}{N}) , depth 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ​ log 2 ​ N ) \mathcal{O}(\log{d}\log^{2}{N}) and width 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ d ​ log ⁡ N ) \mathcal{O}(Nd\log{N}) . ∎

#### H.7.7 The Resulting BaseConv Model

As we have an arithemtic circuit for solving the multiple-query associative recall problem, we can now invoke Theorem H.21 to claim that there is a corresponding BaseConv model that solves the multiple-query associative recall problem with 𝒪 ~ ​ ( N ​ log ⁡ c ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(N\log{c}) parameters and 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) ~ \tilde{\mathcal{O}(1)} layers.

###### Theorem H.37 .

There exists a ( N ​ d , 𝒪 ~ ​ ( 1 ) , 𝒪 ~ ​ ( 1 ) , 𝒪 ~ ​ ( N ​ d ) , 𝒪 ~ ​ ( 1 ) ) − BaseConv \left(Nd,\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1),\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1),\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(Nd),\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1)\right)-\text{{BaseConv}} solves the multiple-query associative recall problem.

###### Proof.

Directly applying Theorem H.21 yields a BaseConv model with the number of layers 𝒪 ⁡ ( log ⁡ d ⋅ log 2 ⁡ N ⋅ log ⁡ N ​ d ​ log ​ N ) = 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathcal{O}(\log{d}\cdot\log^{2}{N}\cdot\log{Nd\log{N}})=\mathcal{O}(1) layers while the claim on the input and inner dimensions follow trivially. ∎

### H.8 Data-Dependent Convolutions

#### H.8.1 Introduction

In this section, we are again concerned with solving the multiple-query associative recall problem ( Section H.7.1 ). However, in contrast to Section H.7.4 , which yields a circuit that is unchanged and works for all inputs, we instead take the viewpoint of adapting the sequence mixing weights ( Section 2 ) with respect to the particular sequence that the model receives as input. More specifically, we take the distance between the tokens in the sequence as a measure for designing data-dependent convolutions.

##### Setup.

To formally setup the problem, as in our discussion of designing a parallel algorithm, we consider the following problem description of the multiple-query associative recall problem. Suppose we are given an input 𝒖 [ 0 ⋯ N − 1 ] ≜ { ( 𝒌 0 , 𝒗 0 , 𝒒 0 ) , … , ( 𝒌 N 3 − 1 , 𝒗 N 3 − 1 , 𝒒 N 3 − 1 ) } \bm{u}[0\cdots N-1]\triangleq\left\{\left(\bm{k}_{0},\bm{v}_{0},\bm{q}_{0}\right),\ldots,\left(\bm{k}_{\frac{N}{3}-1},\bm{v}_{\frac{N}{3}-1},\bm{q}_{\frac{N}{3}-1}\right)\right\} with each 𝒌 i , 𝒗 i , 𝒒 i ∈ C \bm{k}_{i},\bm{v}_{i},\bm{q}_{i}\in C . Here, each token is embedded using the standard one-hot encoding in { 0 , 1 } c \{0,1\}^{c} (i.e. we assume d = c d=c ). 15 15 15 Our arguments do need c = d c=d . However we do not need d = N d=N but we made this simplification for ease of presentation. Our goal is again to check, for each 1 ≤ i ≤ N 3 − 1 1\leq i\leq\frac{N}{3}-1 , whether there exists 0 ≤ j < i 0\leq j<i such that 𝒒 i ≡ 𝒌 j \bm{q}_{i}\equiv\bm{k}_{j} , and if so, output 𝒗 j \bm{v}_{j} . Here, we define the interaction distance between the i i th query 𝒒 i \bm{q}_{i} and the matching key 𝒌 j \bm{k}_{j} as i − j i-j . We then also assume that number of distinct interaction distances is bounded by t t . We can identify these distances using an autocorrelation [ Chatfield, 1995 ] , which has an elegant underlying formulation. We will briefly introduce the relevant mathematical machinery in the context of elucidating the data-dependent model that we seek to develop in the sequel.

##### Autocorrelations

We introduce autocorrelation convolutions. Let 𝒖 ~ ​ [ t ] := 𝒖 ​ [ − t ] \tilde{\bm{u}}[t]:=\bm{u}[-t] , then the cross correlation of two vectors 𝒖 \bm{u} and 𝒗 \bm{v} is given by 𝒖 ⋆ 𝒗 ≜ 𝒖 ~ ∗ 𝒗 . \bm{u}\star\bm{v}\triangleq\tilde{\bm{u}}\ast\bm{v}. The autocorrelation of a vector 𝒖 ∈ ℝ n \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n} is the cross correlation of 𝒖 \bm{u} with itself. Moreover, in terms of polynomials, we have 𝒖 ~ ​ ( X ) = X n − 1 ⋅ 𝒖 ⁡ ( 1 / X ) \tilde{\bm{u}}(X)=X^{n-1}\cdot\bm{u}(1/X) . Thus, in analogy with our interpretation of convolution in terms of polynomial multiplication, we characterize the autocorrelation of a vector 𝒖 ∈ ℝ n \bm{u}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n} , given by 𝐰 ∈ ℝ n {\bf w}\in\mathbbm{R}^{n} as follows: 𝐰 = coeff ⁡ ( 𝒖 ⁡ ( X ) ⋅ 𝒖 ~ ​ ( X ) mod X n − 1 ) . {\bf w}=\mathrm{coeff}\left(\bm{u}(X)\cdot\tilde{\bm{u}}(X)\mod{X^{n}-1}\right).

#### H.8.2 BaseConv with kernels generated using Auto-Correlation

We are now ready to describe the model that solves the multiple-query associative recall problem using data-dependent kernels derived using auto-correlations.

##### The Input-Dependent Kernels.

There are several potential strategies to identify the best token-interaction distances for an input using a convolution. Here we will focus on an autocorrelation convolution for exposition. Autocorrelation allows us to identify the top t t distinct shifts of the sequence that result in highly overlapping values (e.g. matches between query and keys in our associative recall setting). We can then construct convolution filters that perform each of these t t shifts. That is, we define a function 𝚃𝚘𝚙 {\tt Top} such that 𝚃𝚘𝚙 ⁡ ( u ⋆ u , t ) {\tt Top}\left(u\star u,t\right) returns a list of the top t t shifts { s ℓ } ℓ ∈ [ t ] \{s_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[t]} . We then use these top t t distances { s ℓ } ℓ ∈ [ t ] \{s_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[t]} to define the following two kernels: 𝐡 k ​ ( X ) \displaystyle{\bf h}^{k}(X) ≡ ∑ ℓ ∈ [ t ] X s ℓ + ( ℓ − 1 ) ⋅ N 3 , \displaystyle\equiv\sum_{\ell\in[t]}X^{s_{\ell}+(\ell-1)\cdot\frac{N}{3}}, (19) 𝐡 v ​ ( X ) \displaystyle{\bf h}^{v}(X) ≡ ∑ ℓ ∈ [ t ] X s ℓ − 1 + ( ℓ − 1 ) ⋅ N 3 . \displaystyle\equiv\sum_{\ell\in[t]}X^{s_{\ell}-1+(\ell-1)\cdot\frac{N}{3}}. Here, we note that we only only have two kernels as we will assume N ′ = t ​ N 3 N^{\prime}=t\frac{N}{3} and the shift will be done in ”parallel.” Obviously, one can instead define t t distinct shift kernels but then there is a cost of 𝒪 ⁡ ( t ) \mathcal{O}(t) in the number of layers.

##### Projections.

We define the following projections 𝐊 , 𝐐 , 𝐕 ∈ { 0 , 1 } N × d {\bf K},{\bf Q},{\bf V}\in\{0,1\}^{N\times d} of the input that we shall use below using equation 9 . 𝐊 [ i , : ] \displaystyle{\bf K}[i,:] : = { 𝒖 [ i , : ] if ​ i ∈ 𝒦 , 𝟎 d otherwise , \displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\bm{u}[i,:]&\text{if }i\in\mathcal{K},\\ {\bf 0}^{d}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}, (20) 𝐐 [ i , : ] \displaystyle{\bf Q}[i,:] : = { 𝒖 [ i , : ] if ​ i ∈ 𝒬 , 𝟎 d otherwise , \displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\bm{u}[i,:]&\text{if }i\in\mathcal{Q},\\ {\bf 0}^{d}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}, 𝐕 [ i , : ] \displaystyle{\bf V}[i,:] : = { 𝒖 [ i , : ] if ​ i ∈ 𝒱 , 𝟎 d otherwise , \displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\bm{u}[i,:]&\text{if }i\in\mathcal{V},\\ {\bf 0}^{d}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

Finally, we present the BaseConv model that solves the multiple-query associative recall problem with input-dependent kernels using 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathcal{O}(1) layer and 𝒪 ⁡ ( t ⋅ N ​ d ) \mathcal{O}(t\cdot Nd) -many parameters.

###### Theorem H.38 .

There exists a BaseConv model with gated data-dependent convolutions that solves the multiple-query associative recall problem on inputs from { 0 , 1 } 3 ​ N × c \{0,1\}^{3N\times c} with the total number of distinct interaction distances bounded by t t in 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathcal{O}(1) layers and 𝒪 ⁡ ( t ⋅ N ​ c ) \mathcal{O}(t\cdot Nc) total parameters.

###### Proof.

We note that we have the input dimension d = c d=c . Now, for any input sequence 𝒖 ∈ { 0 , 1 } N × d \bm{u}\in\{0,1\}^{N\times d} , we get the input-dependent kernels as in equation 19 using autocorrelation of the input. We will now outline the following computations for the BaseConv layers: 𝒚 \displaystyle\bm{y} = 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 𝐐 ​ ( 𝒖 ) ⊙ ( 𝐡 K ∗ 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 𝐊 ​ ( 𝒖 ) ) \displaystyle={\tt Linear}_{{\bf Q}}(\bm{u})\odot\left({\bf h}^{K}*{\tt Linear}_{{\bf K}}(\bm{u})\right) = 𝐐 ⊙ ( 𝐡 K ∗ 𝐊 ) \displaystyle={\bf Q}\odot\left({\bf h}^{K}*{\bf K}\right) (21) 𝒛 \displaystyle\bm{z} = 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 𝐄 ​ ( 𝒚 ) ⊙ ( 𝐡 V ∗ 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 𝐕 ​ ( 𝒖 ) ) \displaystyle={\tt Linear}_{{\bf E}}(\bm{y})\odot\left({\bf h}^{V}*{\tt Linear}_{{\bf V}}(\bm{u})\right) = 𝐄 ⊙ ( 𝐡 V ∗ 𝐕 ) , \displaystyle={\bf E}\odot\left({\bf h}^{V}*{\bf V}\right), (22) where we have the linear projections 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 𝐐 ​ ( 𝒖 ) = 𝐐 {\tt Linear}_{{\bf Q}}(\bm{u})={\bf Q} , 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 𝐊 ​ ( 𝒖 ) = 𝐊 {\tt Linear}_{{\bf K}}(\bm{u})={\bf K} , 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 𝐕 ​ ( 𝒖 ) = 𝐕 {\tt Linear}_{{\bf V}}(\bm{u})={\bf V} and 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 𝐄 ​ ( 𝒚 ) = 𝐄 {\tt Linear}_{{\bf E}}(\bm{y})={\bf E} defined as 𝐄 [ i , : ] := 𝙻𝚒𝚗𝚎𝚊𝚛 𝐄 ( 𝒚 ) [ i , : ] = { 𝟏 d if ​ ∃ j ∈ [ d ] ​ such that ​ 𝒚 ​ [ i , j ] = 1 𝟎 d otherwise . {\bf E}[i,:]:={\tt Linear}_{{\bf E}}(\bm{y})[i,:]=\begin{cases}{\bf 1}^{d}&\text{if }\exists\ j\in[d]\text{ such that }\bm{y}[i,j]=1\\ {\bf 0}^{d}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}. (23) Here, we will first present the argument for the special case when we have t = 1 t=1 as that will help us elucidate the general case. To this end, as the kernels from equation 19 for t = 1 t=1 are given by 𝐡 k ​ ( X ) \displaystyle{\bf h}^{k}(X) ≡ X s 1 ; \displaystyle\equiv X^{s_{1}}; (24) 𝐡 v ​ ( X ) \displaystyle{\bf h}^{v}(X) ≡ X s 1 − 1 , \displaystyle\equiv X^{s_{1}-1}, we observe that convolving with these kernels 𝐡 ∗ 𝒚 {\bf h}\ast\bm{y} is equivalent to operating with the following primitives ( Section H.3 ): 𝚜𝚑𝚒𝚏𝚝 ​ _ ​ 𝚍𝚘𝚠𝚗 ​ ( 𝒚 , s 1 ) ; \displaystyle\tt{\displaystyle shift\_down}(\bm{y},s_{1}); (25) 𝚜𝚑𝚒𝚏𝚝 ​ _ ​ 𝚍𝚘𝚠𝚗 ​ ( 𝒚 , s 1 − 1 ) . \displaystyle\tt{\displaystyle shift\_down}(\bm{y},s_{1}-1). We note that we shift down instead of shifting up as the index of the top-left entry is ( 0 , 0 ) (0,0) . In order to generalize this to arbitrary t ≤ N 3 t\leq\frac{N}{3} , we first increase the internal dimension so that the input to the kernels 𝒖 ′ ∈ ℝ ( N ⋅ t ) × d \bm{u}^{\prime}\in\mathbbm{R}^{(N\cdot t)\times d} in equation 19 and the projections 𝐊 ′ , 𝐐 ′ , 𝐕 ′ ∈ ℝ ( N ⋅ t ) × d {\bf K^{\prime},Q^{\prime},V^{\prime}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{(N\cdot t)\times d} are given by 𝒖 ′ ≡ ( 𝟎 d 𝟎 d 𝒖 ) , 𝑲 ′ ≡ ( 𝟎 d 𝟎 d 𝐊 ) , 𝐐 ′ ≡ ( 𝐐 𝐐 𝐐 ) , 𝐕 ′ ≡ ( 𝟎 d 𝟎 d 𝐕 ) , \bm{u}^{\prime}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}{\bf 0}^{d}\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ {\bf 0}^{d}\\ \hline\cr\\ \bm{u}\end{pmatrix},\bm{K}^{\prime}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}{\bf 0}^{d}\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ {\bf 0}^{d}\\ \hline\cr\\ {\bf K}\end{pmatrix},{\bf Q}^{\prime}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}{\bf Q}\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ {\bf Q}\\ \hline\cr\\ {\bf Q}\end{pmatrix},{\bf V}^{\prime}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}{\bf 0}^{d}\\ \hline\cr\\ \vdots\\ \hline\cr\\ {\bf 0}^{d}\\ \hline\cr\\ {\bf V}\end{pmatrix}, We then observe that for 𝐡 ℓ k ​ ( X ) := X s ℓ {\bf h}^{k}_{\ell}(X):=X^{s_{\ell}} and 𝐡 ℓ v ​ ( X ) := X s ℓ − 1 {\bf h}^{v}_{\ell}(X):=X^{s_{\ell}-1} , we have 𝐡 k ​ ( X ) \displaystyle{\bf h}^{k}(X) ≡ ∑ ℓ ∈ [ t ] 𝐡 ℓ k ​ ( X ) ⋅ X ( ℓ − 1 ) ⋅ N 3 , \displaystyle\equiv\sum_{\ell\in[t]}{\bf h}^{k}_{\ell}(X)\cdot X^{(\ell-1)\cdot\frac{N}{3}}, 𝐡 v ​ ( X ) \displaystyle{\bf h}^{v}(X) ≡ ∑ ℓ ∈ [ t ] 𝐡 ℓ k ​ ( X ) ⋅ X ( ℓ − 1 ) ⋅ N 3 . \displaystyle\equiv\sum_{\ell\in[t]}{\bf h}^{k}_{\ell}(X)\cdot X^{(\ell-1)\cdot\frac{N}{3}}. \bm{z}_{\ell}[i,:]\equiv\begin{cases}\bm{v}_{i-s_{\ell}}&\text{if }\bm{q}_{i}\equiv\bm{k}_{i-s_{\ell}}\\ {\bf 0}^{d}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases} Finally, we define the last output layer to compute 𝒛 o if ​ 𝒒 i ≡ 𝒌 i − s ℓ ​ for some ​ ℓ ∈ [ t ] 𝟎 d otherwise \bm{z}_{out}[i,:]\equiv\begin{cases}\bm{v}_{i-s_{\ell}}&\text{if }\bm{q}_{i}\equiv\bm{k}_{i-s_{\ell}}\text{ for some }\ell\in[t]\\ {\bf 0}^{d}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases} To recall, we retrieved the top t t interaction distances of the input 𝒖 \bm{u} using auto-correlation and defined the corresponding convolution kernels (equation 19 ). We then shifted djown the keys 𝐊 {\bf K} using the first kernel and gated with the corresponding queries 𝐐 {\bf Q} so that we got a match exactly when there exists a key that is at s ℓ s_{\ell} interaction distance from the corresponding query. After “smearing” this match to get 𝐄 {\bf E} , we used it as a mask to retrieve the value in the next layer. Overall, since we have t t atomic kernels that perform t t shifts with each of these kernels using 𝒪 ⁡ ( N ​ d ) \mathcal{O}(Nd) parameters, we can conclude that the output solves the associative recall problem for all queries with exactly ℓ \ell interaction distance from the corresponding keys for all ℓ ∈ [ t ] \ell\in[t] using 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathcal{O}(1) layers and 𝒪 ⁡ ( t ⋅ N ​ c ) \mathcal{O}(t\cdot Nc) parameters as we have d = c d=c . ∎

## Instructions for reporting errors

We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the methods listed below:

Click the "Report Issue" ( ) button, located in the page header.

Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.

Our team has already identified the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for all.

Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion , and welcome developer contributions .
